be the current set of primitive polynomials that are not super non-singular.
As discussed in
Section

2.3
, in order for the decomposition to be extendible,
one subtlety here is that we do not want to rewrite any of the polynomials
q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}
.
We will refer to them as the
initial polynomials
and the others as the
non-initial polynomials
.
Hence, we want to find a linear combination of the polynomials in
S
such that there is some non-initial polynomial
q_{j}\in S
which has non-trivial weight in the linear combination, i.e., the absolute value of the coefficient in front of
q_{j}
is non-trivial.
Fortunately, by our assumption that the set of initial polynomials are super non-singular,
we know any linear combination of them has non-trivial high-order derivatives with high probability.
Besides, we also have that the derivatives of polynomials are well-concentrated under the Gaussian distribution.
Combining the two observations then allows us to conclude that,
if a linear combination of polynomials from
S
has small high-order derivatives, then there exists some non-initial polynomial from
S
that has non-trivial weight in the linear combination.
The above discussion is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
N,m,\ell,d
be positive integers and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small in
N,m,\ell,d
.
Let
S:=\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
be a set of harmonic polynomials that are of degree at most
d
.
Suppose that
S
is not
(\epsilon,N)
-super non-singular but

\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
is
(\epsilon^{1/3},3(N+1))
-super non-singular.
Furthermore, suppose

\left\|q_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
for all
i\in[m]
.
Denote by
S_{k}
the set of degree-
k
harmonic polynomials in
S
.
Then there exists an algorithm that
runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{m,N}(1/\epsilon)
and finds
a positive integer
k\in[d]
and a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{2}\in(0.9,1.1)
such that

•

The polynomial

q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\;q_{i}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}<\epsilon/2\right]>\epsilon^{N}/2.

(5)

•

There exists
j
such that
j\in S_{k}
,
j>\ell
, and
\mathbf{a}_{j}>\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Since
S
is not super non-singular,
there exists
an integer
k
and a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{2}=1
such that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}<\epsilon\right]>\epsilon^{N}.

(6)

It remains to show that there exists some
j
satisfying
j\in S_{k}
,
j>\ell
, and
\mathbf{a}_{j}>\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
In fact, we will show that there exist
j\in S_{k}
and
j>\ell
such that
\mathbf{a}_{j}>2\;\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
Suppose that there is no such
j
for the sake of contradiction.
Then we claim that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k},i>\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\sqrt{\epsilon})

(7)

with probability at least
1-\epsilon^{N+1}
.
To see this, we note that

\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{k},i>\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2\;(m-\ell)\;\sqrt{\epsilon}
by our assumption that
\left\|q_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
for all
i
and
\mathbf{a}_{j}<2\;\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
By iteratively applying
Fact

3.8
,
this further implies that

\left\|D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\sum_{i\in S_{k},i>\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{d,m}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}\right)
.
Then Equation (
7
) follows from
Lemma

3.17
.

On the other hand, since the set
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
is
(\epsilon^{1/3},3(N+1))
-super non-singular, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\leq\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}>\epsilon^{1/3}

(8)

with probability at least
1-\epsilon^{N+1}
.

By the union bound,

Equation

7
and
Equation

8
are both satisfied with probability at least
1-\epsilon^{N+1}-\epsilon^{N+1}\geq 1-\epsilon^{N}
.
Conditioning on
Equation

7
and
Equation

8
,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\leq\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}-\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k},i>\ell}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon^{1/3}-O_{d,m,N}(\epsilon^{1/2})>\epsilon\,,

where in the first inequality we use the triangle inequality, in the second inequality we use our conditioning, and the last inequality is true as long as
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,m,N
.
This then contradicts
Equation

6
.

The above shows the existence of some
\mathbf{a}
and
k
which satisfy the properties required by the lemma. Though it may be hard to find such an
\mathbf{a}
exactly, we can nonetheless find some
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
that is
\epsilon^{3}
-close to
\mathbf{a}
in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm by searching over all vectors whose coordinates are multiples of
\epsilon^{3}
and lie in the range
[-2,2]
. One can then verify that such a vector still satisfies the properties required by the lemma.
Moreover, for each
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
, checking whether it satisfies the condition in
Equation

5
can be done in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d}\;m/\epsilon^{N})
.
Since there are at most
\mathrm{poly}_{m}(1/\epsilon)
such vector
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
, the overall runtime hence follows.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

4.2
.
∎

As we have discussed, a critical step in obtaining a super non-singular decomposition is to decompose a high-degree polynomial into a set of lower-degree polynomials. To achieve this, it suffices to apply Proposition 10 shown in Section 3.2 of
[
Kan12b
]
.
Roughly speaking, the lemma shows that such a polynomial
q
with small high-order derivatives can be approximately written as the sum of products of lower-degree polynomials such that the
highest degree harmonic part of the error term has a small
L_{2}
norm.

Lemma 4.3

(Proposition 10 from
[
Kan12b
]
)
.

Let
p(\mathbf{x})
be a degree-
d
polynomial with
\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
and let
\epsilon,N>0
be real numbers such that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}p(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}<\epsilon\right]>\epsilon^{N}.

Let
c\in(0,1)
be a real number.
Then there exist
k=O_{N,c,d}(1)
many harmonic polynomials

\{\alpha_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\beta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\}_{i=1}^{k}
of degree
at most
d-1
such that

•

Define
g(\mathbf{x})=p(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}(\mathbf{x})\;\beta_{i}(\mathbf{x})
.
Then it holds

\left\|g^{[d]}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{N,c,d}\left(\epsilon^{1-c}\right)\,,

where
g^{[d]}
denotes the degree-
d
harmonic component of
g
(see
Definition

3.7
).

•

The degrees of
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
are at most
d-1
and they satisfy
\deg(\alpha_{i})+\deg(\beta_{i})=d
.

•

The norms of the lower-degree polynomials satisfy
\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\;\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{N,c,d}\left(\epsilon^{-c}\;\right)\;\left\|p^{[d]}\right\|_{L_{2}}
for all
i\in[k]
.

Finally, such a decomposition can be computed in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{c,d,N}(1/\epsilon)
.

Our algorithm throughout its execution maintains a data structure that we will call a
partial decomposition
.

Definition 4.4

(Partial Decomposition)
.

A partial decomposition consists of the following objects:

•

A positive integer
m
.

•

A sequence of polynomials
Q=(q_{1},\cdots,q_{m})
, to which we refer as the primitive polynomials, with
\left\|q_{i}\right\|_{2}=1
for all
i
. We also require each polynomial to be non-constant.
Over these primitive polynomials, we say
q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}
are the initial polynomials and they are left untouched throughout the process.

•

A set of rational numbers
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{m})
, where each
b_{i}
is
a multiple of
1/6
between
0
and
M+3d
.
We say that
b_{i}
is the
magnitude coefficient
of the primitive polynomial
q_{i}
.
For the initial polynomials, their magnitude coefficients are always fixed to be
0
.

•

A composition polynomial
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. We require that for each monomial
\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\mathbf{\alpha}_{i}}
in
h
, it holds

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\text{deg}(q_{i})\leq d
. The purpose of the composition polynomial is that one can approximately recover the target polynomial
p
by computing

h(\epsilon^{b_{1}}q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,\epsilon^{b_{m}}q_{m}(\mathbf{x})).

When the primitive polynomials in the current partial decomposition are not already super non-singular, we will employ
Lemma

4.2
and
Lemma

4.3
to
replace some non-initial primitive polynomials, i.e., some
q_{j}
for
j>\ell
, with a set of new polynomials with lower degrees.
Such a replacement may create a number of complications.
Specifically, the number of the primitive polynomials may increase, the composition polynomial
h
may become more complex, and the error between the target polynomial
p
and
h(\epsilon^{b_{1}}q_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{b_{m}}q_{m})
may grow larger.
Throughout the iterative process, we need these extra “complexities” to be bounded (otherwise, even storing the decomposition would be intractable).
We hence define an extra complexity parameter for the decomposition to keep track of the above complications.

Definition 4.5

(Complexity of Partial Decomposition)
.

We say that the partial decomposition has complexity
C>0
if the following holds:

•

m\leq C
.

•

\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq C\epsilon^{-3d}
.

•

p(\mathbf{x})=h(\epsilon^{b_{1}}q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,\epsilon^{b_{m}}q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))+\xi(\mathbf{x})
, where
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{b}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is an error polynomial with
\left\|\xi\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq C\epsilon^{M}
. We say that the
L_{2}
-norm of
\xi
is the error of the partial decomposition.

We now give some intuition of why we will make progress after one iteration of replacement.
The high-level idea is that if we repeat the process, we will eventually run out of high-degree polynomials (at least their weights become increasingly small). Hence, the process must eventually terminate and give us a super non-singular decomposition.
To formalize this intuition, we need to carefully define the “potential” of a decomposition. Then we show that this potential decreases monotonically.

Naively, one may want to define the potential of a decomposition as the sum of the magnitude coefficients
b_{i}
, since these numbers intuitively represent the fraction of each polynomial within the decomposition.
However, decreasing the magnitude of one high-degree polynomial may introduce many additional low-degree polynomials.
To make sure the potential is monotonically decreasing after such changes, we need to define the potential to be a weighted sum of
b_{i}
’s.
Specifically, the weights of
b_{i}
’s associated with high-degree polynomials will be much larger than those of
b_{i}
’s associated with low-degree polynomials.

Unfortunately, defining the potential as a sum weighted by
any finite numbers
is still insufficient for the argument to go through.
Similar to the argument for diffuse decompositions in
[
Kan12b
]
,
a technical issue is that
we are not able to bound from above the number of additional polynomials introduced by a fixed number throughout the process.
In fact, the best bound we can put on the increment scales with the complexity parameter
C
of the current decomposition.
To show such a process will eventually terminate, we need to define the weights to be
transfinite ordinal numbers
.
In particular, for
b_{i}
associated with a degree-
k
primitive polynomial, we will weight it by
\omega^{k}
, where
\omega
is the ordinal number defined to be larger than any finite number.

Definition 4.6

(Potential of Partial Decomposition)
.

The potential of a partial decomposition is defined to be a transfinite number — a function of the ordinal number
\omega
(which is bigger than all finite numbers but smaller than
\omega+1
), specifically

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\omega^{\text{deg}(q_{i})}\left(M+3d-b_{i}\right).

Then each replacement indeed causes the potential to become a smaller ordinal number and we can conclude via transfinite induction that the process will eventually terminate.
As a result, the number of iterations needed may only be bounded by some Ackerman-type function of the maximum degree and the number of initial polynomials.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Our goal is to find a decomposition
of the target polynomial
p
such that the primitive polynomials in the decomposition form an
(\epsilon,f(m))
-super non-singular set, where
m
is the size of the set, that
extends the original set of polynomials
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
, which is already
(\sqrt{\epsilon},C_{d,\ell,f,M}(1))
-super non-singular.
We start with an initial partial decomposition with the primitive polynomials being
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1}=p\}
, the composition polynomial being
h(\mathbf{y}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell},\mathbf{y}_{\ell+1})=\mathbf{y}_{\ell+1}
, and the sequence of magnitude coefficients
b_{i}
being
all
0
.

Given a partial decomposition of potential
w
and complexity
C
, we will argue that either the set of primitive polynomials is sufficiently super non-singular already or we can modify it to obtain another partial decomposition with complexity bounded from above by
O_{d,C,f,M}(1)
and strictly smaller potential.
Moreover, the original primitive polynomials
q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}
are left untouched in the new decomposition.
Then we can conclude via transfinite induction that this process will terminate in
O_{d,\ell,f,M}(1)
many steps and the final decomposition obtained will have its complexity bounded by
O_{d,\ell,f,M}(1)
.
Consequently, the size of the final decomposition will be at most some number
m\leq C=O_{d,\ell,f,M}(1)
.
We will argue that each modification can be done in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,C,f,M}(1/\epsilon)
, from which our overall runtime follows.

We proceed to show the inductive step.
In the new decomposition, we may increase some magnitude coefficient
b_{i}
by
1/6
.
Since these numbers are required to be between
0
and
M+3d
, we will perform a preprocessing step to make sure that
b_{i}\leq M+3d-1/6
.
Notice that if
b_{i}=N+3d
,
the sum of coefficients of
q_{i}
appearing in

h(\epsilon^{b_{1}}q_{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{b_{m}}q_{m})

should be at most
O_{C}(\epsilon^{M+3d}\;\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}})\leq O_{C}(\epsilon^{M})
.
Hence, one can create a new partial decomposition by removing these primitive polynomials and the error introduced will be at most
O_{C}(\epsilon^{M})
.
After doing so, we can assume without loss of generality that
b_{i}<M+3d
.

Suppose that the current partial decomposition is of size
m
. We will set
N=f(m)
, and
c
to be some number that is sufficiently small given
N,m,C,d
.
By
Lemma

4.2
, we can find an integer
k
and a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
as specified in the lemma.
Moreover, this can be done in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{m,N}(1/\epsilon)=\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{f,C}(1/\epsilon)
.
Let
S_{t}
be the set of degree-
t
harmonic polynomials within the set of primitive polynomials.
Define the polynomial
q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as

q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x}).

(9)

We therefore have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}<\epsilon/2\right]>\epsilon^{N}\,,

and that there exists
j
such that

j\in S_{k}
,

j>\ell
, and
\mathbf{a}_{j}>\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
Hence, we can apply
Lemma

4.3
to
q
. This shows that we can efficiently find polynomials
\{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\kappa}
, where
\kappa=O_{c,N,d}(1)
, such that the following holds:

1.

Each polynomial
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
is of degree at most
k-1
.

2.

\deg(\alpha_{i})+\deg(\beta_{i})=\deg(q)=k
.

3.

\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\;\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{c,N,d}(\epsilon^{-c})
.

4.

There exist two error polynomials
e,r
with
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{c,N,d}(\epsilon^{1-c})
,
\deg(e)\leq k
and
\left\|r\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{c,N,d}(\epsilon^{-2c})
,
\deg(r)\leq k-1
,
such that

\displaystyle q(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\alpha_{i}(\mathbf{x})\beta_{i}(\mathbf{x})=e(\mathbf{x})+r(\mathbf{x}).

(10)

By
Lemma

4.3
, this can be done in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{c,d,N}(1/\epsilon)=\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{f,C,d}(1/\epsilon)
.
Denote by
\bar{e},\bar{r},\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i}
the normalized versions of the corresponding polynomials.
Our first step is to replace some non-original primitive polynomial
q_{j}
with

\bar{e},\bar{r},\{\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\kappa}
. In particular, the new set of primitive polynomials is set to

q_{1}^{\prime}=q_{1},\cdots,\underline{q_{j}^{\prime}=\bar{e}},q_{m}^{\prime}=q_{m},\underline{q_{m+1}^{\prime}=\bar{\alpha}_{1},q_{m+2}^{\prime}=\bar{\beta}_{1},\cdots,q_{m+2\kappa-1}=\bar{\alpha}_{\kappa},q_{m+2\kappa}=\bar{\beta}_{\kappa},q_{m+2\kappa+1}^{\prime}=\bar{r}}.

Notice that
\kappa=O_{c,N,d}(1)=O_{f,C,d}(1)
. This ensures the
size of the primitive polynomial will not increase by too much.
The polynomial
q_{j}
to be replaced is selected using the following rules:

•

j\in S_{k}
and
j>\ell
.

•

\mathbf{a}_{j}>\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

•

\mathbf{a}_{j}\;\epsilon^{b_{j}}
is the largest among
S_{k}
.

The selection rule will be important when we analyze the complexity of the new decomposition.

We will next update the magnitude coefficient array
b_{1},\cdots,b_{m+2k+1}
:
the magnitude coefficients of the lower degree polynomials
\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i},\bar{r}
are set to
0
, and the magnitude coefficient of
\bar{e}
is set to
b_{j}+1/6
.
Notice that
q_{j}
is of degree
k
,

e
is of degree
k
, and

\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},r
are of degree
k-1
.
Hence, we know in the worst case the potential of the decomposition will change by

\displaystyle-\omega^{k}\left(M+3d-b_{i}\right)+\omega^{k}\left(M+3d-b_{i}-1/6\right)+\omega^{k-1}O_{d,C,f,M}(1)

\displaystyle=-\omega^{k}/6+\omega^{k-1}O_{d,C,f,M}(1).

It is not hard to see that the first term is dominating since
\omega
is larger than any finite number.
This shows that the weight of the decomposition strictly decreases.

Then we will update the composition polynomial.
Using
Equation

9
,
we know that the term
\epsilon^{b_{j}}q_{j}(\mathbf{x})
, which is fed as an input to the original composition polynomial
h
,
can be rewritten as

\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\;q(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\neq j}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x}).

The polynomial
q
, due to
Equation

10
, can be expressed in
\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i},\bar{e},\bar{r}
, which gives us

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\;\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\right)\;\bar{\alpha}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\bar{\beta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+\left(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{-1/6}\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\right)\;\left(\epsilon^{b_{j}+1/6}\bar{e}(\mathbf{x})\right)+\left(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\left\|r\right\|_{L_{2}}\right)\bar{r}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle-\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\neq j}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}-b_{i}}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)\;\left(\epsilon^{b_{i}}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\;.

Recall the new set of primitive polynomials is set to be

q_{1}^{\prime}=q_{1},\cdots,\underline{q_{j}^{\prime}=\bar{e}},q_{m}^{\prime}=q_{m},\underline{q_{m+1}^{\prime}=\bar{\alpha}_{1},q_{m+2}^{\prime}=\bar{\beta}_{1},\cdots,q_{m+2\kappa-1}=\bar{\alpha}_{\kappa},q_{m+2\kappa}=\bar{\beta}_{\kappa},q_{m+2\kappa+1}^{\prime}=\bar{r}}.

Hence, it suffices to replace every occurrence of the
j
-th variable
\mathbf{y}_{j}
in the composition polynomial
h
from last iteration with the expression

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\gamma_{i}\;\mathbf{y}_{m+2i-1}\mathbf{y}_{m+2i}+\theta\mathbf{y}_{j}+\zeta\mathbf{y}_{m+2\kappa+1}+\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\leq\ell}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}+\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\neq j,i>\ell}\iota_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}\,,

(11)

where the coefficients are defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}:=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\text{ indexed by }1\leq i\leq\kappa\,,

\displaystyle\theta:=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{-1/6}\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\,,\,\zeta:=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\left\|r\right\|_{L_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}:=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}-b_{i}}\mathbf{a}_{i}\text{ indexed by }i\in S_{k},i\neq j.

It is easy to see that such a modification on the partial decomposition can be done in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}(m+2\kappa+1)
.
Since we are replacing
\epsilon^{b_{j}}q_{j}(\mathbf{x})
with an equivalent expression, the error of the new decomposition stays the same.

Let
h^{\prime}
be the new composition polynomial obtained after the replacement.
It remains to argue
h^{\prime}
has its
L_{2}
-norm bounded by
C^{\prime}\epsilon^{-3d}
for some
C^{\prime}=O_{C,f,M,d}(1)
.
Due to
Lemma

3.20
, we know it suffices to argue that

\text{MC}(h^{\prime})\leq\epsilon^{-3d}.

Consider a monomial in
h
of the form

A:=\rho\prod_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\tau_{i}}.

Each occurrence of
\mathbf{y}_{j}
in
A
will be replaced with the expression in

Equation

11
.
In other words, the monomial will be replaced with the following expression in
h^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\rho\prod_{i\neq j}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\tau_{i}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\sqrt{\gamma_{i}}\mathbf{y}_{m+2i-1}\;\sqrt{\gamma_{i}}\mathbf{y}_{m+2i}+\theta\mathbf{y}_{j}+\zeta\mathbf{y}_{m+2\kappa+1}+\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\leq\ell}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}+\sum_{i\in S_{k},i\neq j,i>\ell}\iota_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}\right)^{\tau_{j}}.

(12)

We first derive an upper bound for the size of the coefficients appearing in the expression above.

•

For
\gamma_{i},
we have

\displaystyle\left|\gamma_{i}\right|=\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\right|\leq\epsilon^{-1/2-2c}<\epsilon^{-1}\;,

since
\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}\right|>\sqrt{\epsilon}
,
b_{j}\geq 0
, and
\left\|\alpha_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}},\left\|\beta_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\epsilon^{-2c}
.

•

For
\theta
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\theta\right|=\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{-1/6}\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\right|\leq\epsilon^{-1/2-1/6}\;O_{c,d.N}\left(\epsilon^{1-c}\right)\leq 1\;,

where the first inequality follows from our selection rule

\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}\right|>\sqrt{\epsilon}
,
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}<O_{c,d,N}(\epsilon^{1-c})
, and the second inequality is true as long as
c<0.3
and
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
c,d,N
.

•

For
\zeta
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\zeta\right|=\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}}\left\|r\right\|_{L_{2}}\right|\leq O_{c,d,N}\left(\epsilon^{-1/2-2c}\right)\leq\epsilon^{-1}\;,

where the first inequality follows from that
\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}\right|>\sqrt{\epsilon}
,
b_{j}\geq 0
, and

\left\|r\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq O_{c,d,N}(\epsilon^{-2c})
, and the second inequality is true as long as
c<0.2
and
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
c,d,N
.

•

For
\lambda_{i}
and
\iota_{i}
, they are both defined to be

\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}-b_{i}}\mathbf{a}_{i}
.
However, recall that the
\lambda_{i}
’s are defined for
i\leq\ell
and and the
\iota_{i}
’s are defined for
i>\ell
.
For
i\leq\ell
, we always have
b_{i}=0
. Combining this with the fact that
b_{j}\geq 0
,
\mathbf{a}_{i}<1
, and
\mathbf{a}_{j}>\sqrt{\epsilon}
gives us

\left|\lambda_{i}\right|\leq\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\right|\leq\epsilon^{-1/2}.

For
i>\ell
, by our selection rule of
j
, we have that
\mathbf{a}_{j}\epsilon^{b_{j}}
is the largest among all
i>\ell,i\in S_{k}
. This ensures that

\left|\iota_{i}\right|=\left|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon^{b_{j}-b_{i}}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|\leq 1.

Let
B
be some monomial among the ones obtained by expanding
Equation

12
.
Consider the following quantities:

•

u
: the sum of the degrees of the variables
\mathbf{y}_{i}
for
m<i\leq m+2\kappa
. These are the variables that carry a leading coefficient of
\sqrt{\gamma_{i}}
, which is of size at most
\epsilon^{-1/2}
.

•

v
: the degree of the variable
\mathbf{y}_{m+2\kappa+1}
. This is the variable that carries a leading coefficient of
\zeta
, which is of size at most
\epsilon^{-1}
.

•

z
: the difference of the sum of the degrees of the variables
\mathbf{y}_{i}
for
i\in S_{k},i\leq\ell
, in monomial
A
and in monomial
B
. These are the variables that carry a leading coefficient of
\lambda_{i}
, which is of size at most
\epsilon^{-1}
.

Except for the variables mentioned above, the other variables have leading coefficients bounded by
1
. Therefore, one can see that the coefficient of the new monomial
B
obtained from expanding
A
can be bounded from above by

\displaystyle\rho\;\epsilon^{-u/2-v-z}.

(13)

For the monomials
A,B
, consider the following relevant quantities: (i) the degree of the monomial, which we denote by
U(A),V(B)
,
(ii) the degrees of the monomials weighted by the degrees of the corresponding primitive polynomials, i.e.,
V(A):=\sum_{i}\tau_{i}\deg(q_{i})
(and
V(B)
is defined similarly), and
(iii) the degree of the variables corresponding to the original primitive polynomials, i.e.,
Z(A):=\sum_{i\leq\ell}\tau_{i}
(and
Z(B)
is defined similarly.)
We then make the following observations:

•

U(B)=U(A)+u/2
.
This is because the variables
\mathbf{y}_{i}
for
m<i\leq m+2\kappa
in
B
always appear as products of pairs in
Equation

12
in place of
\mathbf{y}_{j}
from
A
.
Hence, if the sum of the degrees of such
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is
u
in
B
, the degree of
B
must be
u/2
larger than the degree of
A
.

•

V(B)\leq V(A)-v
.
To see this, we note that
(i) the degree of
\deg(q_{m+2i-1})+\deg(q_{m+2i})
for
1\leq i\leq\kappa
is precisely
\deg(q_{j})
by
Lemma

4.3
, (ii) the degree of
q_{i}
for
i\in S_{k}
is the same as
q_{j}
since
S_{k}
is defined to be the set of polynomials with degree
k=\deg(q_{j})
and (iii) the degree of
q_{m+2\kappa+1}
is at most
\deg(q_{j})-1
again by
Lemma

4.3
. Hence, if some
\mathbf{y}_{j}
is replaced with
q_{m+2\kappa+1}
, the weighted degree decreases by at least
1
and if
\mathbf{y}_{j}
is replaced with some other terms, the weighted degree remains unchanged.
Consequently, if the degree of
\mathbf{y}_{m+2\kappa+1}
is
v
in
B
, the weighted degree of
B
must have changed by at least
v(\deg(q_{m+2\kappa+1})-\deg(q_{j}))\leq-v
.

•

Z(B)=Z(A)+z
. This follows by the definition of
z
.

Combining the above observations with statement (
13
), we can then use induction to show that the leading coefficient of
B
is at most

\epsilon^{-U(B)+(V(B)-d)-Z(B)}.

The quantities
V(B),U(B),Z(B)
, by their definitions, are all within the range
[0,d]
. Therefore, we conclude that the coefficient of any monomial
B
that appears in
h^{\prime}

is bounded from above by
\epsilon^{-3d}
.
By
Lemma

3.20
, this implies that the
L_{2}
norm of
h^{\prime}

is at most
O_{d,m+\kappa}(\epsilon^{-3d})
.
As the new decomposition indeed has bounded complexity and strictly smaller potential, this finishes the inductive step of the transfinite induction, completing the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.
∎

5
Super Non-Singular Polynomials and (Anti-)Concentration

In this section, we provide the formal statement and the proof of our (anti-)concentration theorem (
Theorem

2.3
) concerning the standard Gaussian distribution conditioned on sets of the form
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R
, where
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a super non-singular transformation and
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is an axis-aligned rectangle.

Theorem 5.1

(Conditional (Anti-)Concentration for Super Non-Singular Transformation)
.

Let
d,\ell,K,C_{d,\ell,K}
be positive integers and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Let
S=\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
be a set of harmonic polynomials, where each
q_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is of degree at most
d
.
Define
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
to be the vector-valued polynomial such that
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})=(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}))
.
Let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be an axis-aligned rectangle satisfying
(i) each point in
R
is at most
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\log(1/\epsilon))
-far from the origin, and
(ii)
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in R\right]>\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
.
Denote by
D
the distribution of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on
\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
.
Suppose
C_{d,\ell,K}
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
, and
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,\ell,K,C_{d,\ell,K}
.
Assume that
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
is
(\epsilon^{1/(3d^{2}K)},C_{d,\ell,K})
-super non-singular.
Then for any polynomial
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
of degree-
d
and
\epsilon<t<\epsilon^{2/K}
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\;\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq t^{1/(2d)}\,,

(14)

and, for all
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\epsilon^{-1/K}\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,K}\left(t^{-K}\right).

(15)

We first outline our proof plan.
To show the above theorem, we will first use
Theorem

4.1
to extend
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
into a super-non singular decomposition of the target polynomial
p
. In particular, this gives us a super non-singular set of polynomials
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
such that
h(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))\approx p(\mathbf{x})
.
Define
\bar{\mathbf{q}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
to be
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})=(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))
.
Since
p(\mathbf{x})
and
h(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))
are approximately the same, the polynomial
p
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
will roughly have the same (anti-)concentration properties as
h
under
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
.

Then it remains to show
h
has good (anti-)concentration properties under
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
.
To achieve the goal, we need to first prove a structural result showing that
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is close in total variation distance to some distribution comparable (see
Definition

2.5
) to a mixture of reasonable Gaussians (see
Definition

2.4
).
Specifically, we establish the following.

Proposition 5.2

(Super Non-Singular Polynomial Transformations are Reasonable)
.

Let
m,d,N,C_{m,d,N}
be positive integers, where
C_{m,d,N}
is sufficiently large given
m,d,N
.
Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be sufficiently small given the aforementioned integers.
Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a
(\delta^{1/(3d)},C_{m,d,N})
-super non-singular vector-valued polynomial of degree
d

8
8

8

Recall that we say a vector-valued polynomial is super non-singular if it consists of a super non-singular set of real-valued polynomials.
.
Then the distribution
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to
some distribution that is comparable to some mixture distribution

\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
, where each
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}

is a
\left(\delta,\log^{O(d)}(1/\delta)\right)
-reasonable Gaussian.

Gaussian distributions are still log-concave if we condition on an axis-aligned rectangle
R
, therefore still satisfying decent (anti-)concentration properties after the conditioning.
It turns out this is also roughly true for a mixture of reasonable Gaussians (see
Lemma

5.18
) conditioned on an axis-aligned rectangle that is not too far from the origin (which is assumed in
Theorem

5.1
).
With
Lemma

5.18
and
Proposition

5.2
established, it is then not hard to conclude that the polynomial
h
has good (anti-)concentration properties under
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
.

Sections
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.3
are devoted to the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.
After that, we discuss the (anti-)concentration properties of (mixture of) reasonable Gaussians (
Lemma

5.18
) in
Section

5.4
.
In Sections
5.6
,
5.7
, and
5.8
, we conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, where we transfer the (anti-)concentration properties from a mixture of reasonable Gaussians conditioned on
R

to
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
conditioned on
R
, and then finally to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on
\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
.

5.1
Non-Singularity of Jacobian

As we have already mentioned, the key idea to establish
Proposition

5.2
is the observation that the polynomial transformation behaves somewhat like a non-singular linear transformation.
As our starting point, we show that the super non-singularity condition implies that the Jacobian of the polynomial transformation evaluated at
\mathbf{x}
must be non-singular with high probability when
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})

9
9

9

As a matter of fact, this condition on the Jacobian of a polynomial transformation is equivalent to the exact definition of non-singularity used in the work of
[
Kan12a
]
.
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
m,d,N,C_{m,d,N}
be integers, where
C_{m,d,N}
is sufficiently large given
m,d,N
.
Let
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}|q^{(i)}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
be a set of
(\delta^{1/d},C_{m,d,N})
-super non-singular polynomials.
Define
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
to be vector-valued polynomial whose
i
-th coordinate is given by
q_{i}
.
Then it holds

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\sigma_{\min}\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\right)\leq\delta\right]<\delta^{N}.

Our overall strategy for showing
Lemma

5.3
is by contradiction.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\sigma_{\min}(\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})<\delta)\right]>\delta^{N}

(16)

We start with the simple observation that

Equation

16
is essentially equivalent to

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\min_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|_{2}=1}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\sum_{i=1}\mathbf{a}_{i}\;q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}<\delta\right]>\delta^{N}\;.

(17)

In order to obtain a contradiction against the definition of super non-singularity, there are still two issues we need to tackle:

1.

The linear combination coefficients
\mathbf{a}_{i}
in
Equation

17
which minimize

\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}
are allowed to be different for different values of
\mathbf{x}
, while the same coefficients are used across all different
\mathbf{x}
in the definition of super non-singularity (
Definition

2.2
).

2.

Super non-singularity poses requirements on the
k
-th order derivatives of the subset of polynomials of degree
k
, while
Equation

17
poses one requirement on the first-order derivatives of the entire set of polynomials.

To tackle the first issue, we consider a finite cover of the set of unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. If we further condition on the event that the gradients of the initial polynomials are themselves not too big (which follows from standard Gaussian concentration), we can conclude that at least one vector from the cover will be effective for a set of
\mathbf{x}
with non-trivial at mass under the Gaussian distribution at the same time.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
N,m
be positive integers and
\delta\in(0,1)
be sufficiently small given
N,m
.
Suppose that
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
is such that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\min_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|_{2}=1}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\sum_{i=1}\mathbf{a}_{i}\;q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}<\delta\right]>\delta^{N}.

(18)

Furthermore, suppose that

\left\|q_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
for all
i
in
[m]
.
Then there exists a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

with
\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|_{2}=1

such that the polynomial

q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\;q_{i}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies

\pr\left[\left\|\nabla q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq 2\;\delta\,,\,\left\|\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m}(\log^{2d}(1/\delta))\text{ for all }i\right]\geq\delta^{N+m+1}.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.17
and union bound, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\log^{d/2}(1/\delta))\;\forall\;i\right]\geq 1-\delta^{N}/2.

(19)

Again, by the union bound, both
Equation

18
and
Equation

19
are true with probability at least
\delta^{N}/2
over
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
Conditioned on that, there exists some vector
\mathbf{a}
such that

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta.

Now consider a new vector
\mathbf{\tilde{a}}
that is obtained by rounding each
\mathbf{a}_{i}
to an integer multiple of
\delta/\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)
:

\mathbf{\tilde{a}}_{i}=\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{a}_{i})\;\lceil\frac{\left|\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)}{\delta}\rceil\;\frac{\delta}{\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)}.

One can verify that

\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{a}}\right\|_{2}\geq\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|_{2}=1
and

\left\|\mathbf{a}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{2}\leq\delta\;\sqrt{m}/\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)
.
Conditioned on the event that
\left\|\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\log^{d/2}(1/\delta))
for all
i\in[m]
,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta+O_{d,m,N}\left(\log^{d/2}(1/\delta)\right)\;\left\|\mathbf{a}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\left(1+O_{d,m,N}(\log^{-1}(1/\delta))\right)\leq 2\delta\;,

where in the first inequality we use the triangle inequality, in the second inequality we use our upper bound for
\left\|\mathbf{a}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{2}
and the last inequality is true as long as
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,N
.
Note that there are at most
(2\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)/\delta+2)^{m}
many vectors whose coordinates are all integer multiples of
\delta/\log^{d/2+1}(1/\delta)

and lie in the range
[-1-\delta/\log^{2d+1}(1/\delta),1+\delta/\log^{2d+1}(1/\delta)]
.
Therefore, there exists some
\mathbf{a}^{*}
(independent of the choice of
\mathbf{x}
) such that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}^{*}_{i}\;q_{i}(\mathbf{x})/\left\|\mathbf{a}^{*}_{i}\right\|_{2}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq 2\delta\,,\,\left\|\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\log^{2d}(1/\delta))\text{ for all }i\in[m]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta^{N}}{2}\;(2\log^{2d+1}(1/\delta)/\delta+2)^{-m}=\delta^{N+m}/\mathrm{poly}_{d,m}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)\geq\delta^{N+m+1}\,,

where the last inequality follows from our assumption that
\delta^{-1}
is a sufficiently given
d,m,N
.
∎

To tackle the second issue, we need the following lemma which relates first-order derivatives of a polynomial to its higher-order derivatives.

Lemma 5.5

(Proposition 11 from
[
Kan12b
]
)
.

Let
c,N>0
be real numbers and
d
be a positive integer.
Let
\delta>0
be a real number that is sufficiently small given
c,N,d
.
Suppose that
q
is a degree-
d
polynomial such that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\nabla q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}<\delta\right]>\delta^{N}\;.

Then we have that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}}q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}<\delta^{1-c}\right]>\delta^{O_{N,c,d}(1)}\;.

Equipped with the above tool, we can iteratively apply it to the polynomial
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}
, where
\mathbf{a}
is the global linear combination coefficient obtained through
Lemma

5.4
.
Then there are two cases to consider.
In the first case, the sum of the coefficients
\left|\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|
, where
q_{i}
is one of the highest degree polynomials in the set is sufficiently large. In this case, it suffices to apply
Lemma

5.4

d-1
many times and the contributions from the other low-degree polynomials in the linear combination will all vanish;
this contradicts the definition of super non-singularity.
In the second case, we can discard the highest degree polynomials from the linear combination and recurse on the remaining polynomials.
We are now ready to present the formal proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

Let
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
be a set of polynomials with unit
L_{2}
norm that does not satisfy
Equation

17
.
We will show that there exists a number
C_{d,N,m}
that depends only on
d,N,m

such that the set is not
(\delta^{1/d},C_{d,N,m})
-super non-singular either.
Recall that for the set to be super non-singular, each polynomial needs to be harmonic. Hence, we assume that
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
are all harmonic since otherwise the proof is trivial.
Applying
Lemma

5.4
gives us that there exists some
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|_{2}=1
such that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x}))\right\|_{2}\leq 2\delta\,,\,\left\|q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\log^{2d}(1/\delta))\forall i\right]\geq\delta^{N}\;.

(20)

Let
S_{t}
be the set of degree-
t
harmonic polynomials within
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
.
Define
w_{t}
as the weight assigned to each set
S_{t}
by the vector
\mathbf{a}
, i.e.,
w_{t}=\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{2}}
.
Let
k
be the largest integer such that

w_{k}\geq\delta^{k/d}
.
Such a
k
must exist because there exists
t
such that
w_{t}\geq 1/\sqrt{d}\gg\delta^{1/d}
.
Consider the polynomial

q
obtained by removing the contributions from
S_{t}
for
t>k
.
That is, we can write:

q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{t=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q(\mathbf{x})\;.

We claim that this polynomial satisfies

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\nabla q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq 3\delta\right]\geq\delta^{N}\;.

This is because for each
\mathbf{x}
which satisfies the condition in
Equation

20
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{t=k+1}^{d}\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{a}_{i}\nabla q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}+\sum_{t=k+1}^{d}\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\left|\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|\left\|q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+O_{d,m,N}\left(\log^{d/2}(1/\delta)\right)\;\sum_{t=k+1}^{d}\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\left|\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|\;,

where in the first inequality we use the triangle inequality, and in the second inequality we use the condition in
Equation

20
.
Now we claim the sum
\sum_{t=k+1}^{d}\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\left|\mathbf{a}_{i}\right|
is bounded from above by

O_{m}\left(\delta^{(k+1)/d}\right)
.
This is because the inner summation is at most

O_{m}\left(\delta^{t/d}\right)
since the group will only be removed if
w_{t}=\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S_{t}}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{2}}<\delta^{t/d}
. Then we can bound from above the entire summation as a geometric sequence dominated by
k=d+1
and conclude that it is at most
O_{m}\left(\delta^{(k+1)/d}\right)
. Thus, we further have

\left\|q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq 2\delta+O_{d,m,N}\left(\log^{d/2}(1/\delta)\delta^{(k+1)/d}\right)\leq\delta^{(k+0.75)/d}\,,

where the last inequality is true as long as
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,N
.

Now we can choose some
c
that is sufficiently small given
k,d
and then iteratively apply
Lemma

5.5

k-1
times on
q
.
This gives us

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}q(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{(k+0.5)/d}\right]\geq\delta^{O_{d,m,N}(1)}\;.

(21)

We note that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}q(\mathbf{x})=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\,,

(22)

since the contributions from the lower degree polynomials in
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
disappear after the
k
derivative operations.
Thus, combining

Equation

21
and

Equation

22
gives us

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1}\left[\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{(k+0.5)/d}\right]\geq\delta^{O_{d,m,N}(1)}\;.

The only remaining issue is that
\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{2}}=w_{k}
instead of equal to
1
.
Nonetheless, we have chosen
k
such that
w_{k}\geq\delta^{k/d}
.
Hence, we can normalize
\mathbf{a}
to obtain another vector
\bar{\mathbf{a}}
with
\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}^{2}}=1
and still have that

\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}D_{\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}}\cdots D_{\mathbf{y}^{(1)}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{k}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{(k+0.5)/d-k/d}=\delta^{1/(2d)}

with probability at least
\delta^{O_{d,N,m}(1)}
over the randomness of

\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{y}^{(k-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
This clearly violates the definition of
(\delta^{1/(2d)},C_{d,N,m}(1))
-super non-singularity for some number
C_{d,N,m}
that depends only on
d,N,m
.
∎

5.2
Approximate Linear Transforms

When the underlying polynomials are of higher-degrees, the transformed distribution defined by them may no longer have a nice form.
However, as the Jacobian of the polynomial transformation is non-singular almost everywhere (
Lemma

5.3
), this transformation will jointly behave like some linear transformation if one restricts to “local” areas. In fact, in this section, we will show that the distribution of
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is close to a mixture of
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
, where each transformation
\mathbf{p}
is “approximately” linear.
Notice that if each
\mathbf{p}
were exactly linear, each transformed distribution in the mixture would indeed be some reasonable Gaussian and we would be nearly done.

Below we formalize the notion of an approximately linear transform.
One point worth noticing is that the original vector-valued polynomial
\mathbf{q}
maps an
n
-dimensional vector to an
m
-dimensional vector where
m
can be much smaller than
n
.
In the special case where
\mathbf{q}
is exactly linear, one can see that the mapping only retains a “fraction” of the randomness in the input variable
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
In particular, the transformed distribution is equivalent to
some linear transformation of an
m
-dimensional standard Gaussian.
We hence formalize our definition of an approximate linear transform as an
\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
map.

Definition 5.6

(Degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform)
.

We say that
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate-linear transform if there exist:

•

a vector
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\left\|\mathbf{g}\right\|_{2}\leq\log^{b}(1/\delta)
,

•

a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
with its singular values bounded by

\delta\leq\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq\log^{b}(1/\delta)
,

•

a vector-valued polynomial
\mathbf{r}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
of degree
d
satisfying

\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{5/4}
10
10

10

We remark that the choice of the constant
5/4

is just to simplify the computation in some parts our proof.
For our downstream application, we only require it to be a constant slightly larger than
1
.
,

such that

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x})\;.

The main technical lemma of this section is given below.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
d,m,N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
Let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small in
d,m,N
.
Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a degree-
d
vector-valued polynomial satisfying

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\sigma_{\min}\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\right)<\delta^{1/2}\right]<\delta^{N}\;.

(23)

Then the distribution of
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is

O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to an (infinite) mixture of
distributions of the form

\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
, where

\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a
degree-
d
,
(d+1)
-bounded,

\delta^{3/2}
-approximate-linear transform.

We begin with the simple observation that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is equivalent to the distribution of

\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})
, where
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}
are independently distributed as
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
Our intuition is that, due to the non-singularity condition,
\mathbf{q}
will behave like some linear transformation locally.
To show this formally, we will need a Taylor-style expansion of the expression

\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})
. We begin with the simpler case for a scalar polynomial
q
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a degree-
d
harmonic polynomial with
\left\|q\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
, and let
\delta\in(0,1/4)
.
Then there exists degree-
d
polynomials
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and
e:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with

\left\|g\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
,
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2^{d/2}\delta^{2}

such that

q(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})=g(\mathbf{x})+\left(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\right)^{d-1}\;\delta\;\nabla q(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{z}+e(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\,,

for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 5.9
.

For the sake of clarity, we note that the expansion resembles the Taylor expansion of
q(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})
viewed as a function of
\delta\mathbf{z}
around
\mathbf{0}
, but the two expansions are not exactly the same. In particular, if it were exactly the Taylor expansion, the coefficient in front of the linear term would be
\nabla q(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x})
instead of
\nabla q(\mathbf{x})
. However, we do need the gradient term to be evaluated at
\mathbf{x}
exactly to exploit the non-singularity property of
\mathbf{q}
under the standard Gaussian distribution.
Due to this subtle difference, we need to use properties of Hermite polynomials to establish the expansion needed carefully instead of applying Taylor’s Theorem in a black-box manner.

Proof.

We assume that

q(\mathbf{x})
is a harmonic polynomial of degree
d
with Hermite decomposition

q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}H_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x})
.
Applying
Fact

3.9
then gives

\displaystyle q(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s}}\sqrt{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{\left|\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}\right|}\delta^{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{d}\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-j}\delta^{j}\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left|\mathbf{k}\right|=j}\sqrt{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\;.

Notice that

H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{z})

and
H_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{z})

will be orthogonal if either
\mathbf{s}\neq\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
or
\mathbf{k}\neq\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
.
Hence, this is an orthogonal decomposition of the polynomial
q(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})
.
We will analyze the summation for the terms
j>1
,
j=1
, and
j=0
separately.
Define

\displaystyle e(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{d}\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-j}\delta^{j}\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left\|\mathbf{k}\right\|_{1}=j}\sqrt{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\,,

(24)

\displaystyle m(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-1}\delta^{1}\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left\|\mathbf{k}\right\|_{1}=1}\sqrt{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{x})\;H_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{z})\,,

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d}\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}H_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x})\,,

which correspond to the terms with
j>1
,
j=1
,
j=0
respectively.

For
e
, we show that it is a polynomial with
L_{2}
-norm at most
2^{d/2}\delta^{2}
.
Exploiting the fact that
Equation

24
is an orthogonal decomposition of

e(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
,
we have the squared
L_{2}
-norm of
e
is given by

\displaystyle\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{d}\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left\|\mathbf{k}\right\|_{1}=j}\left(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-j}\delta^{j}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sqrt{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\;\sum_{j=2}^{d}(1-\delta^{2})^{d-j}\delta^{2j}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left|\mathbf{k}\right|=j}{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{1}=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\;\delta^{4}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left\|\mathbf{k}\right\|_{1}\geq 2}{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}\,,

where in the inequality we use the elementary fact

(1-\delta^{2})^{d-j}\delta^{2j}\leq\delta^{4}

for
j\geq 2
and
0<\delta<1
.
Now for the summation

\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s},\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\geq 2}{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}^{2}
,
we claim that it can be bounded above by
2^{d}
.
In particular, it is bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k}\leq\mathbf{s}}{\binom{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{k}}}^{2}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mathbf{k}_{i}=0}^{\mathbf{s}_{i}}{\binom{\mathbf{s}_{i}}{\mathbf{k}_{i}}}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n}2^{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\,,

where in the last inequality we use the Binomial theorem. Since we also have the restriction that
\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d
, it follows that the expression is bounded above by
2^{d}
.
Since
\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}=\left\|q\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq 1
, it follows that

\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2^{d/2}\delta^{2}
.

For the
j=1
term, we can simplify the expression as

\displaystyle m(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-1}\delta\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sum_{i:\mathbf{s}_{i}>0}\sqrt{\mathbf{s}_{i}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{\mathcal{I}}(i)}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbf{z}_{i}\,,

where we use
\mathcal{I}(i)
to denote the indicator vector that is
1
on the
i
-th coordinate and
0
everywhere else.
Notice that
\sqrt{\mathbf{s}_{i}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathcal{I}(i)}(\mathbf{x})
is exactly the partial derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}_{i}}H_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x})
.
Accordingly, for a fixed coordinate
i
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{s}:\left|\mathbf{s}\right|=d}\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{s}}\sqrt{\mathbf{s}_{i}}H_{\mathbf{s}-\mathcal{I}(i)}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}_{i}}q(\mathbf{x})\;.

Summing over all coordinates
i
then gives

m(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-1}\;\delta\;\nabla q(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{z}\;.

Finally, for
j=0
, it is easy to see that

g(\mathbf{x})=\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d}q(\mathbf{x})\;.

Hence,
\left\|g\right\|_{L_{2}}<\left\|q\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
.
∎

As an immediate corollary, we obtain a generalized version of the above lemma for vector-valued polynomials.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
be a set of real-valued harmonic polynomials of degree at most
d
.
Define
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
to be the vector-valued polynomial such that
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})=(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))

denote the vector-valued polynomial

\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=q^{(i)}(\mathbf{x})
.
Assume that
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
.
Then it holds

\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})+\delta\;\mathbf{D}^{(\mathbf{q})}\;\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\;,

where

\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is a diagonal matrix such that
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}_{i,i}=\left(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\right)^{\deg(q_{i})-1}
for
i\in[m]
,

\left\|\mathbf{g}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
,
and
\left\|\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2^{d/2}\;\delta^{2}
.

Proof.

The result follows by applying
Lemma

5.8
to each polynomial
\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})
.
∎

To focus on “local” behaviors of the transformed distribution
\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})
, we can condition on
\mathbf{x}
being some fixed value
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
and view
\mathbf{q}\left(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}^{\ast}+\delta\mathbf{z}\right)
as a function of
\mathbf{z}
.
Then using
Corollary

5.10
, it is not hard to check that the linear component dominates over the higher order terms in
\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{z})
while viewed as a function of
\mathbf{z}
, as long as the value
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
ensures that
\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})
is non-singular and satisfies some other mild technical conditions.
Since
\mathbf{q}
consists of a non-singular set of polynomials, we know that
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
indeed satisfies the conditions with high probability.
The detailed argument is given below.

Proof of
Lemma

5.7
.

Instead of analyzing the distribution of
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})

for
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})

directly, we consider the distribution of

\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z})

for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}
being two i.i.d. random variables from
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
It is easy to see that the two distributions are the same since the distribution of
\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{z}
is still
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.

Using
Corollary

5.10
,
we have

\mathbf{q}(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\;\mathbf{x}+\delta\;\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})+\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}^{d-1}\;\delta\;\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\;\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}).

We will first condition on
\mathbf{x}
being some fixed value
\mathbf{x}^{*}
that satisfies the following:

1.

\left\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m}\;N^{d}\log^{d}(1/\delta)
.

2.

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{m}\;N^{d}\log^{d}(1/\delta)
.

3.

The singular values of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*})
are lower bounded by
\delta^{1/2}
.
Notice that this immediately implies that
\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\;\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right)\geq\Omega(\delta^{1/2})

4.

\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{z})
as a function of
\mathbf{z}
has small
L_{2}
-norm, i.e.,

\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{z})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\leq\delta^{1.9}.

Recall that
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is a diagonal matrix, where
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}_{i,i}=\left(\sqrt{1-\delta^{2}}\right)^{\deg(q_{i})-1}
for
i\in[m]
as specified in
Corollary

5.10
.
When
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d\geq\deg(q_{i})
, we then have
(1-o(1))\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\preceq\mathbf{I}
. Denote
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}:=\mathbf{D}^{\mathbf{q}}\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})
.
We thus have
\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\right\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{m}N^{d}\log^{d}(1/\delta)
and

\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\right)\geq\delta^{1/2}/2
.

Notice that we condition on
\mathbf{x}
being some
fixed
value
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
that satisfies the above restrictions rather than conditioning on
\mathbf{x}
satisfying the conditions.
After such a conditioning, the randomness comes from
\mathbf{z}
solely.
Now consider the row space of

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}
.
We will next decompose
\mathbf{z}
in terms of its projection in the row space and in the orthogonal complement of the row space.
Since
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}
is non-singular, the row space is
m
-dimensional.
Let
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
be a matrix obtained by concatenating a set of orthonormal vectors from the row space of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}
, and

\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(n-m)}
be a matrix obtained by concatenating a set of orthonormal vectors from the orthogonal complement of the row space of
\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})
.
Then the distribution of
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n})
is the same as

\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\;\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\;\mathbf{v}

for
\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{m})
and
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})
.
Therefore, the distribution after the conditioning on
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
is equivalent to

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})+\delta\;\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\;\left(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\mathbf{v}\right)+\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\mathbf{v})\,,

where
\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{m})
and
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})
.
Notice that we always have

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\mathbf{v}=0
by the definition of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}
.
We claim the
L_{2}
-norm of
\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\mathbf{v})
as a function of
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}
is the same as the
L_{2}
-norm of
\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{z})
as a function of
\mathbf{z}
.
This is because

\displaystyle\E_{\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{m}),\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})}\left[\mathbf{e}^{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\mathbf{v})\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{m}),\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})}\left[\mathbf{e}^{2}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\left[\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{v}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[\mathbf{e}^{2}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\left[\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}\right]\mathbf{z}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[\mathbf{e}^{2}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{z}\right)\right]\,,

where the last equality follows from the fact that

[\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}^{\perp}]
is a unitary matrix.
Therefore, the distribution after the conditioning on
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
is equivalent to

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})+\delta\;\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\;\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})

for some degree-
d
polynomial
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
with
L_{2}
-norm bounded above by
\delta^{1.9}
.

We next condition on
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
such that

\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\ast})
as a function of
\mathbf{u}
has
L_{2}
-norm bounded by
\delta^{1.875}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},I_{m})}\left[\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\ast})\right]}\leq\delta^{1.875}.

(25)

We will denote the new function after fixing
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
as

\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}^{\ast}}
.
The distribution after the conditioning is equivalent to

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})+\delta\;\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\;\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u})\,,

for
\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},I_{m})
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}
has the (compact) singular value decomposition

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\Sigma_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\,,

where
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
.
Since both
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
are semi-unitary, one can verify that
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})
is some unitary matrix of dimension
\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
.
Hence,
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
is some
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
whose singular values are bounded below by
\sqrt{\delta}/2
(since its singular values are exactly those of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}
).
Furthermore, since

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}\right\|_{F}
is at most

\sqrt{m}N^{2d}\log^{2d}(1/\delta)
, it follows that the singular values of
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
are also bounded above by
\sqrt{m}N^{2d}\log^{2d}(1/\delta)
.
Hence, we conclude that the distribution after the conditioning of
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
is that of

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})+\delta\;\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u})\,,

(26)

where
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, and
\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}^{\ast}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

We know that
\left\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m,N}(\log^{d}(1/\delta))
by our choice of
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
. This is indeed bounded above by
\log^{d+1}(1/\delta)
when
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,N
.
We have just shown that the singular values of
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
are between
\delta
and
O_{d,m,N}\left(\log^{d}(1/\delta)\right)<\log^{d+1}(1/\delta)
. Hence, the singular values of
\delta\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}
should be between
\delta^{3/2}
and
\delta\log^{d+1}(1/\delta)
.
For
\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}^{\ast}}
, our choice of
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
guarantees that its
L_{2}
-norm is at most
\delta^{1.875}=\left(\delta^{3/2}\right)^{5/4}
.
This verifies that the transformation in
Equation

26
is indeed a degree-
d
,
(d+1)
-bounded,
\delta^{3/2}
-approximate linear transform.

It remains to argue that
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})

satisfies the criteria of
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
(specified at the beginning of the proof) with probability at least
1-O(\delta^{N})
, and for each valid
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
,

\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})
satisfies the criteria of
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
(
Equation

25
) with probability at least
1-O(\delta^{N})
.
If so, it follows that the overall distribution is
O(\delta^{N})
close in total variation distance to a mixture of distributions of the desired form.

Concentration of valid
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
.

We will examine the criteria for
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
one by one and argue that each criterion fails with a small probability for
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
We start with the condition
\left\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m}N^{d}\log^{d}(1/\delta)
.
For each of the coordinates, we have

\left\|\mathbf{g}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
.
This implies that

\left\|\mathbf{g}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq m.

Then using
Lemma

3.16
, we have

\left\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d}(1)\;N^{d/2}\;\log^{d/2}(1/\delta)\;\left\|L_{2}\right\|_{2}{\mathbf{g}}\leq\sqrt{m}\;N^{d}\;\log^{d}(1/\delta)\,,

with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
.

For
\left\|\mathbf{J}_{q}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\right\|_{2}
, since
\left\|\mathbf{q}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
for each
i
, we can use
Lemma

3.18
and conclude that

\left\|\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{F}\leq N^{d}\log^{d}(1/\delta)\sqrt{m}\,,

with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
.

Next, the singular values of
\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{\ast})
are bounded from below by
\sqrt{\delta}
with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
by our assumption that
\mathbf{q}
satisfies
Equation

23
.

Finally,
we wish to show that

\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\leq\delta^{1.9}\,,

with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
over the randomness of

\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
Our starting point is that

\left\|\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2^{d/2}\;\delta^{2}
.
It follows that

\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left\|\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}\leq\sqrt{m}\;2^{d/2}\;\delta^{2}.

Define the restricted function

\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{e}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
.
Using
Lemma

3.19
, we have

\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left\|\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{z})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\leq N^{d}\;\log^{d}(1/\delta)\;\sqrt{m}\;2^{d/2}\;\delta^{2}\,,

with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
over the randomness of
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
One can check that the right-hand side is indeed bounded by
\delta^{1.9}
when
\delta
is sufficiently small given
N,d,m
.

Concentration of
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
.

The proof is similar to the argument of the last criterion of
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
.
We already have that
\left\|\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{1.9}.

Define the restricted function

\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
.
By
Lemma

3.19
, it holds

\left\|\mathbf{r}_{\mathbf{x}^{\ast},\mathbf{v}}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\mathrm{poly}_{N,d,m}(\log(1/\delta))\;\delta^{1.9}\leq\delta^{1.875}

with probability at least
1-\delta^{N}
over the randomness of
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{n-m})
.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.7
.
∎

5.3
Comparing to Reasonable Gaussians

Note that the approximate linear transforms constructed via

Lemma

5.7
are not exactly linear due to an extra polynomial error factor.
Consequently, applying these approximate linear transforms on Gaussian inputs will not give distributions that are exactly Gaussian.
However, as we require the
L_{2}
-norm of the super-linear polynomial component to be significantly smaller than the minimum singular value of the linear component, the resulting distribution will be close in total variation distance to some distribution
comparable
(see
Definition

2.5
for the notion of distribution comparability) to a reasonable Gaussian distribution (see
Definition

2.4
for the definition of a reasonable Gaussian). After that, we will combine
the structural result of this section (
Lemma

5.11
) with
the tools developed in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
to conclude the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

Consider an approximate linear transform
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}

of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\,,

(27)

where
\mathbf{g}
is the offset vector,
\mathbf{M}
is the linear transformation matrix and
\mathbf{r}
is the residual polynomial, all satisfying the conditions of
Definition

5.6
with appropriate parameters.
The main goal of this subsection is to show that

\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
will be close to some distribution whose probability density function is within constant factors of the probability density function of some Gaussian distribution.
In particular, we will show it is close to some distribution that is comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
in total variation distance. Moreover, by the assumptions that
\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}
satisfy the conditions of
Definition

5.6
, it is not hard to see that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
is a reasonable Gaussian with appropriate parameters.
The formal statement, which is the main result of this subsection, is given below.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then the distribution
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}
-close in total variation distance to some distribution that is comparable to some
(\delta,\log^{2b}(1/\delta))
-reasonable Gaussian.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of
Lemma

5.11
.

Analyzing the probability density function of
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
at points far away from the origin is quite challenging due to the influence of the polynomial component.
To circumvent this issue, we will exploit the fact that
\mathbf{p}
is invertible within some origin-centered ball
B
of radius bounded by
\mathrm{poly}_{d,b}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)
.
That way, using the change of variable formula of probability density functions, we are at least able to compute explicitly the probability density function of
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
, where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B
represents the normal distribution truncated to the set
B
.
Then if we can show that
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
is comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
,
Lemma

5.11
will then follow from the fact that
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
and
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
are close in total variation distance.
In particular, since
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
are close in total variation distance when the radius of
B
is sufficiently large, it should not be hard to conclude that the original transformed Gaussian
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))

is close to being comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
as well.
In the rest of this subsection, for convenience, we will use the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\,,\,\mathcal{\bar{M}}:=\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)\,,

(28)

where
B
is an origin-centered ball of radius
\ell:=\log(1/\delta)
.

We start by showing that
\mathbf{p}
is locally invertible. Moreover, as one would expect, its inverse will be close to that of its linear part.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform in
Equation

27
.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then
\mathbf{p}
is invertible within some origin-centered ball
B
with radius
\log(1/\delta)
. Moreover, if we denote by
\mathbf{p}^{-1}
the inverse of
\mathbf{p}
restricted to the ball
B

11
11

11

As
\mathbf{p}
is not necessarily invertible globally, from this point on, we always mean the inverse of
\mathbf{p}
restricted to
B
if we write
\mathbf{p}^{-1}
.
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{1/8}.

(29)

Proof.

Let
\ell=\log(1/\delta)
, and
B(\ell)
be the origin-centered ball centered with radius
\ell
.
By definition, we can write the transformation as

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\,,

where
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m},\mathbf{r}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfy the conditions specified in
Definition

5.6
.
To show that
\mathbf{p}
is invertible on the restricted domain

B(\ell)
, it suffices to show that for any
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}\in B(\ell)
,

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})\neq\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{v})
.
Using the definition of
\mathbf{p}
, we have that

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{M}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\right)+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{v}).

On the one hand,
since the singular values of
\mathbf{M}
is bounded below by
\delta
,
we have that

\left\|\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})\right\|_{2}\geq\delta\left\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\right\|_{2}.

On the other hand, we know that

\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{5/4}
and

\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in B_{m}(\ell)
.
Therefore, by
Corollary

3.24
, we have that

\left\|\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{v})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{d,m}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}\;\left\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\right\|_{2}.

Hence, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{v})\right\|_{2}\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\right\|_{2}\,,

(30)

when
\delta
is sufficiently small given
m,b,d
.
This then implies that
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})\neq\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{v})
for
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}
, and hence concludes the argument for the invertibility of
\mathbf{p}
within
B
.

We next show that for
\mathbf{y}
within the image of
B_{m}(\ell)
under
\mathbf{p}
,
it holds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{1/8}.

By definition, there exists
\mathbf{w}\in B_{m}(\ell)
such that

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w}).

Then the left hand side of
Section

5.3
can be simplified as

\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})-\mathbf{g})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}.

Since the smallest singular value of
\mathbf{M}
is bounded below by
\delta
, it holds
\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{-1}
.
