On the other hand, by
Corollary

3.22
, we have that
\left\|\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{m,d}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}.

It then follows that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{m,d}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{1/4}\leq\delta^{1/8}\,,

when
\delta
is sufficiently small compared to
m,d,\ell
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

5.12
.
∎

After knowing that
\mathbf{p}
is locally invertible over some ball
B
of bounded radius, we can apply the change of variables formula to deduce the probability density function of the transformed distribution
\bar{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbf{p}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B\right)
.
That is,
for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{p}(B)
, we have

\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\left(\sqrt{2\pi}\right)^{m}}\;\frac{1}{\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in B\right]}\;\exp\left(-\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\;\frac{1}{\det\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}))\right)}.

We would like to compare
\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})
to the probability density function of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
:

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})=\frac{1}{\left(\sqrt{2\pi}\right)^{m}}\;\exp\left(-(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g})^{T}\left(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g})\right)\frac{1}{\det(M)}\,,

where we denote by
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
the probability density function of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
evaluated at
\mathbf{y}
.
Specifically, we would like to show that
\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})
is pointwise within a constant factor of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{p}(B)
.
It is not hard to see that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in B\right]
is close to
1
, since the radius of
B
is sufficiently large.
Moreover, the expressions within the exponential function are also close to each other due to the approximate linearity of
\mathbf{p}^{-1}
shown in
Lemma

5.12
.
Hence, it remains to show that the determinant of the Jacobian of
\mathbf{p}
is close to that of
M
.
In fact, we will show a stronger property: the singular values of the two matrices are close up to a multiplicative factor of at most
1\pm\mathrm{poly}(\delta)
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform in
Equation

27
,
\ell=\log(1/\delta)
, and
B
be an origin-centered ball of radius
\ell
.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then for all
\mathbf{x}\in B
it holds

(1-O(\delta^{0.1}))\sigma_{i}(M)\leq\sigma_{i}(\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x}))\leq(1+O(\delta^{0.1}))\sigma_{i}(M).

Consequently, if
\delta
is sufficiently small given
m
, it holds

\displaystyle 0.5\det(M)\leq\det\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{x})\right)\leq 2\det(M).

(31)

Proof.

By
Equation

33
, we have

\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}\left(\mathbf{w}\right)=\mathbf{M}+\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w})\,,

where
M
is a matrix whose singular values are bounded below by
\delta
, and

\mathbf{r}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies

\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{5/4}
.
By
Corollary

3.23
, we have

\left\|\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{F}\leq O_{d,m}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}.

It follows that all the singular values of
\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w})
are also bounded above by
O_{d,m}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}
.
Therefore,
using Weyl’s inequality (
Fact

3.1
) for singular values, we have

\left|\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}}+\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w}))-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}})\right|\leq\sigma_{\max}(\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w}))\leq O_{m,d}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}\leq\delta^{1.1}\,,

where the last inequality follows from our choice of
\delta
to be sufficiently small.
As
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\geq\delta
,
we have

\frac{\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M}+\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{w}))}{\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})}=(1\pm O(\delta^{0.1})).

It then follows that

\det(\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{w}))=\left(1\pm o(1)\right)\det(M).

This completes the proof of
Lemma

5.13
.
∎

With
Lemma

5.12
and
Lemma

5.13
, we can then bound the ratio between the probability density functions of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
and
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform in
Equation

27
,
\ell=\log(1/\delta)
,
B
be an origin-centered ball of radius
\ell
, and
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
be the distribution defined in
Equation

28
.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then for all
\mathbf{y}
within the support of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
,
it holds

(1-O(\delta^{0.1}))\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})\leq\bar{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{y})\leq(1+O(\delta^{0.1}))\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}).

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{\bar{M}}=\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
.
Therefore,
the support of
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
is exactly
\mathbf{p}(B)
.
By
Lemma

5.12
,
\mathbf{p}
is invertible over
B
.
Hence, we can apply the change of variable formula of probability density function.
In particular, this gives us that for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{p}(B)
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\;\frac{1}{\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in B\right]}\;\frac{1}{\det\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}))\right)}\exp\left(-\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).

The term
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in B\right]
is at least
1-\delta^{\log(1/\delta)}
by our choice of
\ell
. The Jacobian term is of order

\left(1\pm o(1)\right)\det^{-1}(M)
by
Lemma

5.13
.
It remains to analyze the expression inside the exponential function.
By
Lemma

5.12
, the inverse function is approximately linear. Hence, we can write

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(M^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)+\mathbf{\xi}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)^{T}\left(M^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)+\mathbf{\xi}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}\left(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}+2\;\mathbf{\xi}_{\mathbf{y}}^{T}\;M^{-1}\;\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)+\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\,,

where the term
\xi_{\mathbf{y}}
is some error term depending on
\mathbf{y}

with
\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{1/8}
.
Except for the first quadratic term that is standard in the probability density function of a normal distribution, we need to argue the two remaining terms are both small.
For the constant term, since
\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{y}}\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{1/8}
, it immediately follows that the term is of order
o(1)
.

For the middle linear term, we need to take advantage of the fact that
\mathbf{y}
is a vector from the set
\mathbf{p}(B)
.
By definition, we know that there exists
\mathbf{w}\in B
such that

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\,,

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\;\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\leq\ell+\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\,,

where we first apply the triangle inequality and in the second inequality we bound
\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|_{2}
by
\ell
since
\mathbf{w}\in B
, which is a ball of radius
\ell
.
Recall that
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{5/4}
by our assumption.
Hence, using
Corollary

3.22
, we have that

\left\|\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\leq O_{m,d}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\delta^{5/4}=o(\delta).

By the assumption that
\mathbf{p}
is a
degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform, we have that
\sigma_{\max}\left(\mathbf{M}^{-1}\right)=1/\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathbf{M}\right)\leq\delta^{-1}
.
Hence, it follows that

\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\;\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\ell+\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{2}\leq\ell+o(1).

This further implies that

2\;\mathbf{\xi}_{\mathbf{y}}^{T}\;M^{-1}\;\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)\leq O(\ell\;\delta^{1/8})=o(1)
.
Hence, overall, we have

\displaystyle\exp\left(\left\|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}\left(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}\pm o(1)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1\pm o(1)\right)\;\exp\left(\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}\left(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{g}\right)^{T}\right).

Combining the above gives that for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{p}(B)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=(1\pm o(1))\;\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.14
.
∎

At this point, it is tempting to conclude that the distribution
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
is comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
.
However, we emphasize that the above lemma applies only for points
within the support of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
.
Unfortunately, the supports of the two distributions are not aligned.
Yet, recall that our final goal is only to show closeness to some distribution comparable to the Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
in total variation distance.
Hence, the final missing piece is to show that the support of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
(which is the image of
B
) under
\mathbf{p}
) is not too “light”.
More accurately, we need to show that the points outside the support of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
have negligible mass under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
.
To show this, we require some basic understanding of what the set
\mathbf{p}(B)
looks like.
By our assumption,
\mathbf{p}
is approximately a linear map. If
\mathbf{p}
were exactly a linear map,
\mathbf{p}(B)
would then be an ellipsoid.
The fact that
\mathbf{p}
is only approximately linear makes the exact characterization of
\mathbf{p}(B)
difficult.
Nonetheless, we manage to show below that
\mathbf{p}(B)
still
includes
an ellipsoid.
Specifically, it includes the ellipsoid
\mathbf{M}B^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}
, where
B^{\prime}
is a ball of slightly smaller radius than
B
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform in
Equation

27
, and
B
be an origin-centered ball of radius
\ell=\log(1/\delta)
.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then the image of
B
under
\mathbf{p}
includes the ellipsoid
\mathbf{M}B^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}
, where
B^{\prime}
is an origin-centered ball of radius
0.9\ell
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.15
is similar to that of Lemma 1.3 in Chapter XIV of
[
Lan12
]
.
There, the author shows that if a function
\mathbf{f}
is close of being the identity mapping, then the image of a ball under
\mathbf{f}
will include a ball of only a slightly smaller radius.
The proof strategy is the following.
Suppose that one wants to show that a point
\mathbf{y}
within this ball of a smaller radius belongs to the image of some ball
B
under the function.
One can then use
\mathbf{y}
, the function, and its derivatives to construct a contraction mapping from
B
to itself and demonstrate the fixed point of the mapping is exactly
\mathbf{y}
.
In our setting, our function
\mathbf{p}
is only close to being a non-singular linear transformation.
Nonetheless, it turns out that one can use Newton–Raphson’s iteration to construct a contraction mapping and mimic the proof strategy of Lemma 1.3 in
[
Lan12
]
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.15
.

We will show the following. For all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}B^{\prime}
, we can find
\mathbf{x}\in B
such that

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{y}
.
Consider the mapping
T_{\mathbf{y}}:B\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined using a slightly modified Newton-Raphson iteration:

T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{y}\right)\,,

where
\mathbf{M}
is the non-singular matrix in the linear term in
Equation

33

12
12

12

One can see that
\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})
is very close to
\mathbf{M}
. Hence, this is almost the standard Newton–Raphson’s iteration.
.
We will show that the map is a contraction on
B
.
In particular, for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in B
, we claim that

\displaystyle\left\|T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})-T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq o(1)\;\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}.

Using the definition of
T_{\mathbf{y}}
, we can write

\displaystyle\left\|T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})-T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{M}\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)-\left(\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\right)\right\|_{2}.

(32)

By the intermediate value theorem, there exists some

\mathbf{\tilde{x}}=t\mathbf{x}+(1-t)\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
for
t\in(0,1)
such that

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right).

We can then further rewrite
Equation

32
as

\left\|T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})-T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{M}-\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})\right)\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\right\|_{2}.

On the other hand, since
\mathbf{\tilde{x}}\in B
,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{M}-\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})\right\|_{F}=\left\|\Jac_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})\right\|_{F}\leq O_{d,m}(1)\;\ell^{d}\;\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}<o(\delta)\,,

where in the equality we use the defining equation of
\mathbf{p}
(
Equation

33
),
in the first inequality we use
Corollary

3.23
, and the second inequality is true since
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{5/4}
and
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Since the operator norm of matrices is sub-multiplicative, we then further have

\left\|T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})-T_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}(M^{-1})\;\sigma_{\max}\left(\mathbf{M}-\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})\right)\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}<o(1)\;\left\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}\,,

since the singular value of
M
is bounded below by
\delta
and the singular value of
\mathbf{M}-\Jac_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{\tilde{x}})
is bounded above by
o(\delta)
.
It then follows that
T_{\mathbf{y}}
is actually a contraction mapping from
B
to itself.
By the contraction mapping theorem, there is a fixed point of
T_{\mathbf{y}}
.
Lastly, since
M^{-1}
is non-singular, it is easy to see that the fixed point
\mathbf{x}^{*}
must satisfy
f(\mathbf{x}^{*})=\mathbf{y}
.
This concludes our proof.

∎

Then we can easily bound the mass of the points outside the support of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
via standard Gaussian concentration argument.

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the degree-
d
,
b
-bounded,
\delta
-approximate linear transform in
Equation

27
and
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
be the distribution defined in
Equation

28
.
Furthermore, assume that
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,b
.
Then it holds

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})}\left[\mathbf{x}\not\in\text{support}(\bar{\mathcal{M}})\right]\leq\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}.

Proof.

Let
B^{\prime}
be an origin-centered ball of radius
0.9\ell
.
By
Lemma

5.15
,
it suffices to argue that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{M}B^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}\right]\geq 1-\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}.

Notice that, by applying the transformation
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbf{M}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{g}\right)
, the left hand side is equal to

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in B^{\prime}\right].

If
m=1
, deriving a lower bound of this probability boils down to standard Gaussian tail bounds.
For
m>2
, we show that this situation is not much different when
\delta
is sufficiently small given
m
.

Claim 5.17
.

It holds

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{m})}\left[\left\|\mathbf{x}\right\|_{2}\geq v\right]\leq\exp\left(-\Omega(v^{2})\right)

when
v
is sufficiently large given
m
.

Proof.

The above is equivalent to
\left\|\mathbf{x}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq v^{2}
and
\left\|\mathbf{x}\right\|_{2}^{2}
is exactly the
\chi^{2}
distribution with mean
m
.
By the tail bound of the
\chi^{2}
distribution, we hence have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2}\geq\frac{v^{2}}{m}\;m\right]\leq\left(\frac{v^{2}}{m}\;\exp\left(1-\frac{v^{2}}{m}\right)\right)^{m/2}\leq\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\ell^{2}\right)\right)\,,

where the last inequality is true as long as
v
is sufficiently large given
m
.
∎

Applying
Claim

5.17
with
v=0.9\ell
then concludes the proof of
Corollary

5.16
.
∎

Now, we are ready to conclude the proof of
Lemma

5.11
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.11
.

By definition, we can write the transformation as

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{w})\,,

(33)

where
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m},\mathbf{r}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfy the conditions specified in
Definition

5.6
.
It follows from the definition that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
is a
(\delta,\log^{2b}(1/\delta))
-reasonable Gaussian.
Our goal is to show that the transformed distribution of
\mathbf{p}
applied to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
is close to some distribution
comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
.

It is challenging to analyze the behavior of the transformed distribution
\mathcal{M}=\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))

in regions that are far away from the origin.
On the other hand, we know that
\mathbf{p}
is locally invertible within some origin-centered ball with bounded radius by
Lemma

5.12
.
For this purpose, we will restrict our attention to some origin-centered ball
B
of radius
\ell:=\log(1/\delta)
.
This allows us to consider the distribution
\bar{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
instead.
Using the data-processing inequality for total variation distance, we know that the total variation distance between

\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B)
and
\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
can be bounded above by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{T}V}\left(\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B),\mathbf{p}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\right)\leq d_{\mathrm{T}V}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid B,\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\right)\leq\delta^{\Omega\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)}\,,

(34)

where in the second inequality we again use
Claim

5.17
with
v=\ell
.

By
Lemma

5.14
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=(1\pm o(1))\;\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right).

Consider the positive measure
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})&\text{ if $\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{p}(B)$}\,,\\
\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

On the one hand, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle 1-o(1)\leq\frac{\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)}\leq 1+o(1)

(35)

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
On the other hand,

\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
only differ on the complement of the support of
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
. By
Corollary

5.16
, the mass of the points outside the support of
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
is at most
\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
.
Hence, if we denote by
\mathcal{K}
the support of
\mathcal{\bar{M}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{y}}\left|\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{\bar{M}}(\mathbf{y})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\pr_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)}\left[\mathbf{y}\not\in\mathcal{K}\right]\leq\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}.

(36)

Denote by
\mathcal{\hat{D}}
the distribution obtained by normalizing the measure
\mathcal{D}
.
One can check Inequalities (
35
) and (
36
) still hold if we replace
\mathcal{D}
with
\mathcal{\hat{D}}
.
In summary, this gives us a distribution
\mathcal{\hat{D}}
such that

0.5\leq\frac{\mathcal{\hat{D}}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T}\right)}\leq 2

and that

d_{\mathrm{T}V}\left(\mathcal{\hat{D}},\mathcal{M}\right)\leq d_{\mathrm{T}V}\left(\mathcal{\hat{D}},\mathcal{\bar{M}}\right)+d_{\mathrm{T}V}\left(\mathcal{\bar{M}},\mathcal{M}\right)\leq\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}.

It then follows that the original transformed distribution
\mathcal{M}
is
\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}
-close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{\hat{D}}
, which is comparable to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{T})
.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.11

∎

With the tools developed in the past three sections, we are finally able to conclude the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

By the assumption in
Proposition

5.2
,
\mathbf{q}
is a
\left(\delta^{1/(3d)},C_{d,m,N}\right)
-super non-singular polynomial transformation of degree at most
d
.
By
Lemma

5.3
, we know that
\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is non-singular with high probability. In particular, we have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\sigma_{\min}\left(\Jac_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\right)<\delta^{1/3}\right]<\delta^{N}.

By
Lemma

5.7
, we hence have that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to some mixture distribution

\int_{\theta}\mathbf{p}_{\theta}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))d\theta
, where each
\mathbf{p}_{\theta}
is a degree-
d
,
(d+1)
-bounded,
\left(\delta^{1/3}\right)^{2\;\frac{3}{2}}=\delta
-approximate linear transform.
By
Lemma

5.11
, each
\mathbf{p}_{\theta}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}
-close in total variation distance to some distribution
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
that is comparable to
a
\left(\delta,\log^{2(d+1)}(1/\delta)\right)
-reasonable Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
.
Hence, when
\log(1/\delta)
is a sufficiently large multiple of
N
, it follows that

\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
O(\delta^{N})+\delta^{\Omega(\log(1/\delta))}=O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}d\theta
, which is comparable to
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
.
This concludes the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.
∎

5.4
Reasonable Gaussians

Using
Proposition

5.2
, we have reduced the proof of
Theorem

5.1
to show (anti-)concentration properties of mixture of reasonable Gaussians. This goal will be accomplished in the current subsection. The formal statement of the main result of this subsection is given below.

Lemma 5.18

((Anti-)Concentration of Mixture of Reasonable Gaussians)
.

Let
d,\ell,m
be positive integers where
\ell<m
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
\kappa>1
.
Let
\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
be a mixture of
(\delta,\kappa)
-reasonable Gaussians in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a degree-
d
polynomial.
Let
I_{1},\cdots,I_{\ell}
be a set of intervals such that each interval is at most
\kappa
-far from the origin.
Let
R
be the axis-aligned rectangle
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}

13
13

13

Notice that the last
m-\ell
coordinates of
R
are left unrestricted.
.
Then for any
t>0
, we have the anti-concentration bound

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R}[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}]<O_{d,m}(1)\;\left(t\delta^{-2d}\kappa^{6d}\right)^{1/d}\,,

and the concentration bound

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\Omega_{d,m}\left(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d}t\right)^{1/d}\right).

We now give the proof overview of
Lemma

5.18
.
Gaussian distributions conditioned on the convex set
R:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}
are log-concave distributions.
Hence, Carbery-Wright Theorem (
Lemma

3.14
) and the polynomial concentration lemma (
Lemma

3.15
) directly implies that each component within the mixture will satisfy good (anti-)concentration properties.
The main issue is that the “scale” of each distribution is different.
More specifically, the
L_{2}
-norms of the target polynomial
h
under different Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
conditioned on
R
may be different.
Hence, the main step towards establishing anti-concentration of the conditional Gaussian mixture is to argue that the
L_{2}
-norms of the target polynomial under different reasonable conditional Gaussians are within a factor of

\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,b}(\delta)
from each other.

As a warm up, we show that for low-dimensional reasonable Gaussians (without any conditioning), the
L_{2}
-norms of
h
under these distributions will be bounded by a factor of
\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,b}(\delta)
from each other.
This is due to the fact that the
L_{2}
-norm of a polynomial under a reasonable Gaussian is the same as the
L_{2}
-norm of the polynomial composed with some linear transformation under the standard Gaussian.
As we have shown in
Lemma

3.20
,
the
L_{2}
-norm of a low-dimensional and low-degree polynomial under the standard Gaussian is roughly proportional to its maximum coefficient.
Therefore, as long as the linear transformation does not change the maximum coefficient of the polynomial by too much, the bound will follow.
Specifically, we show the following:

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
be a
(\delta,\delta^{-1})
-reasonable Gaussian and
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a degree-
d
polynomial.
Then it holds

\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}),L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{-d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}.

Proof.

Define the degree-
d
polynomial
h^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as

h^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=h(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{\mu}).

By the definition of a reasonable Gaussian, we have that

\delta\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{\Sigma}\preceq\delta^{-1}\mathbf{I}\,,\,\left\|\mathbf{\mu}\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{-1}.

This further implies that

\Theta_{d,m}\left(\delta^{d}\right)\text{MC}(h)\leq\text{MC}(h^{\prime})\leq\Theta_{d,m}\left(\delta^{-d}\right)\text{MC}(h).

On the other hand, we have that

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}),L_{2}}=\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L_{2}}
.
Combining this with the fact that
\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L_{2}}=\Theta_{d,m}(\text{MC}(h^{\prime}))
, and
\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}=\Theta_{d,m}(\text{MC}(h))
(
Lemma

3.20
) then yields the bound in the lemma.
∎

Next we try to analyze the effect of conditioning on a reasonable Gaussian.
Though the conditioning considered in
Lemma

5.18
involves intervals, we will consider the more extreme case where a subset of coordinates are conditioned to be equal to some specific values.
After such extreme conditioning, the resulting distribution becomes a lower-dimensional Gaussian, and hence no longer reasonable. However, it is not hard to show that if we isolate out the coordinates used for conditioning, the marginal distribution on the remaining coordinates remains a reasonable Gaussian.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
d,\ell,m
be positive integers, where
\ell<m
.
Let
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
be a
(\delta,\kappa)
-reasonable Gaussian in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be a vector such that
\mathbf{a}_{i}<\kappa
.
Then
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
conditioned on the set
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}
is a
(\delta,\delta^{-d}\kappa^{d})
-reasonable Gaussian.

Proof.

We first leverage the block decomposition of
\mathbf{\mu}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\mu}_{1}\\
\mathbf{\mu}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\,,\,\mathbf{\Sigma}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\\
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}\,,

(37)

where
\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the mean of the
k
coordinates being conditioned,
\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-k}
is the mean of the remaining coordinates, and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(m-k)\times(m-k)}
are the corresponding sub-covariance matrices.
Since
\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}\right)>\delta
, the same holds for
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}
.
Notice that the marginal distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
under the conditioning is some distribution whose
last
m-k
coordinates form
another
(m-k)
-dimensional Gaussian distribution

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\tilde{\mu}},\mathbf{\tilde{\Sigma}})

with parameters

\displaystyle\mathbf{\tilde{\mu}}=\mathbf{\mu}_{1}+\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\;\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}^{-1}\;(\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{\mu}_{2})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\tilde{\Sigma}}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}-\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}^{-1}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}.

We proceed to bound the parameters.
Since
\left\|\mathbf{\mu}\right\|_{2}\leq\kappa
and

\delta\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{\Sigma}\preceq\kappa\mathbf{I}
,
the entries in
\mathbf{\mu}_{1}
,
\mathbf{\mu}_{2}
,
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}
,
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}
,
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}
are all bounded above by
\kappa
and the entries in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}^{-1}
are bounded above by
\delta^{-1}
.
It then follows that
\left\|\tilde{\mu}\right\|_{2}\leq O_{m}(\delta^{-1}\;\kappa^{2})
.
For
\mathbf{\tilde{\Sigma}}
, we remark that its expression is exactly the Schur complement of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Therefore, we can employ the following fact.

Fact 5.21
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
.
Suppose

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Sigma}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\\
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}\,,

(38)

where
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(m-k)\times(m-k)}
.
Denote by
\mathbf{S}
the Schur complement

\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,1}-\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,2}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,2}^{-1}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2,1}.

If
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is positive definite, then it holds

\lambda_{t}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\geq\lambda_{t}(\mathbf{S})\geq\lambda_{t+k}(\mathbf{\Sigma}).

This then gives us

\delta\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{\tilde{\Sigma}}\preceq\kappa\mathbf{I}.

We can thus conclude that the marginal of the conditional distribution is a
(\delta,O_{m}(\delta^{-1}\kappa^{2}))
-reasonable Gaussian.
∎

As we have mentioned in the proof overview of
Lemma

5.18
, the main step is to show the
L_{2}
norms of
h
under the mixture of conditional Gaussians are roughly at the same scale.
We next combine
Lemma

5.19
and
Lemma

5.20
to establish the above intuition formally.
Specifically, we show that:

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
be a mixture of
(\delta,\kappa)
-reasonable Gaussians and
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a polynomial of degree
d
.
Let
I_{1},\cdots,I_{\ell}
be a set of intervals that are at most
\kappa
far from the origin and
R
be the set of vectors defined as
R:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}
.
Then for any reasonable Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{*}}
within the mixture, it holds

\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d})\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{*}}|R,L_{2}}\leq\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d})\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{*}}|R,L_{2}}.

Proof.

From the definition of the mixture, we know that
\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}^{2}
is a linear combination of
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R,L_{2}}^{2}d\theta
, where
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
is a reasonable Gaussian in the mixture.
For a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, define the set
R(\mathbf{a})

as
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}
.
Then for a reasonable Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
, we have that

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R,L_{2}}^{2}

is a linear combination of

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R(\mathbf{a}),L_{2}}^{2}
, where
\mathbf{a}\in I_{1},\cdots,I_{\ell}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that for any two
(\delta,\kappa)
-reasonable Gaussians
\mathcal{N}_{\theta},\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}

and any two vectors
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in I_{1},\cdots,I_{\ell}
, it holds

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d})\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R(\mathbf{a}),L_{2}}\leq\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}|R(\mathbf{a}^{\prime}),L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{-2d}\kappa^{6d})\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R(\mathbf{a}),L_{2}}.

(39)

For a vector
\mathbf{a}
, we can define the restricted polynomial function

h_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbb{R}^{m-\ell}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as
h_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{x}):=h(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x})
.
We will also denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\theta,\mathbf{a}}
the

(m-\ell)
-dimensional marginal distribution of the last
m-\ell
coordinates of
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mid R(\mathbf{a})
.
Then it is not hard to see that

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R(\mathbf{a}),L_{2}}=\left\|h_{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta,\mathbf{a}},L_{2}}.

(40)

By
Lemma

5.20
,
\mathcal{N}_{\theta,\mathbf{a}}
is a
(\delta,O_{m}(\delta^{-1}\kappa^{2}))
-reasonable Gaussian.
Hence, applying
Lemma

5.19
, we have

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{d}\kappa^{-2d})\;\left\|h_{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\left\|h_{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta,\mathbf{a}},L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{-d}\kappa^{2d})\left\|h_{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{L_{2}}.

(41)

Since the size of each coordinate of
\mathbf{a}
is bounded above
\kappa
, we have

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,m}(\kappa^{-d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\left\|h_{\mathbf{a}}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\kappa^{d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}.

(42)

Combining
Equation

40
,
Equation

41
, and
Equation

42
then gives us

\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{d}\kappa^{-3d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R(\mathbf{a}),L_{2}}\leq\Theta_{d,m}(\delta^{-d}\kappa^{3d})\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}.

Equation

39
hence follows, completing the proof of
Lemma

5.22
.
∎

We are ready to conclude the proof of our main lemma in this subsection.

Proof of
Lemma

5.18
.

Define
R
to be the set of vectors
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[\ell]\}
.
We start with the anti-concentration bound.
For any
t>0
and each reasonable Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
in the mixture, applying the Carbery Wright Theorem (
Lemma

3.14
) gives us that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq O(dt^{1/d}).

Applying
Lemma

5.22
gives us

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<\Theta_{d,m}\left(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d}\right)t\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq O(t^{1/d}).

By reparametrizing the variable
t
in the equation, we know it is equivalent to

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,m}\left(\left(t\delta^{-2d}\kappa^{6d}\right)^{1/d}\right).

Since this holds for each Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
in the mixture, the anti-concentration of the entire mixture hence follows.

Now we turn our attention to the concentration bound.
For any
t>0
and each reasonable Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
in the mixture, applying
Lemma

3.15
gives us

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq\exp(-\Omega(t)^{1/d}).

Applying
Lemma

5.22
gives us

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\;\Theta_{d,m}\left(\delta^{-2d}\kappa^{6d}\right)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq\exp(-\Omega(t)^{1/d}).

By reparametrizing the variable
t
in the equation, we know it is equivalent to

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\theta}|R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R,L_{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\Omega_{d,m}\left(\delta^{2d}\kappa^{-6d}t\right)^{1/d}\right).

This completes the proof of
Lemma

5.22
.
∎

5.5
Proof of
Theorem

5.1
Global Definitions

The current subsection and the remaining three subsections
Section

5.6
,
Section

5.7
, and
Section

5.8
are all devoted to conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.
For that reason, throughout the rest of the section, we are going to repeatedly use some variables and mathematical objects.
To avoid restating their definitions in different places, we will define these variables and objects globally at the beginning of this subsection.

Global Parameters

\epsilon,\delta
will be used to denote real numbers in
(0,1)
and

d,\ell,K,M,N,C_{K,d,\ell}
will be used to denote positive integers.
Their sizes are required to satisfy the following conditions.

M,N
will be numbers that depend on
K,d,\ell
only and they are required to be sufficiently large given
K,d,\ell
.

C_{K,d,\ell}
will be some sufficiently large function of
K,d,\ell,M,N
(which is simply some other sufficiently large function of
K,d,\ell
, since
M,N
depends only on
K,d,\ell
).
The parameters
\delta,\epsilon
satisfy the equality
\delta^{d^{2}K}=\epsilon
, and

\epsilon
is required to be sufficiently small given all the aforementioned integers (which is simply some sufficiently small function of
d,\ell,K
).

Global Mathematical Objects

The mathematical objects used will be as follows:

•

\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
are a
\left(\epsilon^{1/(3d^{2}K)}=\delta^{1/3},C_{K,d,\ell}\right)
-super non-singular set of polynomials.

•

\{I_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a set of intervals such that each
I_{i}
is at most
\mathrm{poly}_{d}(\log(1/\epsilon))
-far from the origin.

•

\mathcal{P}
is defined to be the set
\mathcal{P}:=\{\mathbf{x}:q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\in I_{i}\forall i\in[\ell]\}
and
R
is defined to be the axis-aligned rectangle

R:=I_{1}\times I_{2}\cdots\times I_{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}^{m-\ell}
.

•

D
is the distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on
\mathcal{P}
.

•

p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is the given target polynomial of degree
d
. We are interested in showing concentration and anti-concentration properties of
p(D)
.
Since these properties are invariant up to scaling, we assume without loss of generality that
\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}=1
.

•

The vector-valued polynomial
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the composition polynomial
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and the error polynomial
e:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}

are constructed as follows.
Since we assume that the set of polynomials
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
is
\left(\epsilon^{1/(3d^{2}K)},C_{K,d,\ell}\right)
-super non-singular and

C_{K,d,\ell}
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,M,N
, we can use
Theorem

4.1
to show that for some
m=O_{d,\ell,M,N}(1)=O_{K,d,\ell}(1)
there exists a
(\delta,N)
-super non-singular set of polynomials

\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
, a composition polynomial
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, and an error polynomial
e:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{M}

such that

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{x})=h(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))+e(\mathbf{x}).

(43)

We define
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
as the vector-valued polynomial whose
i
-th coordinate is
q_{i}
.

•

\mathcal{M}
and
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
are distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. They are constructed as follows.
Using
Proposition

5.2
, we know that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to some distribution
\mathcal{M}
that is comparable to a mixture of
(\delta,\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log(1/\delta)))
-reasonable Gaussians, i.e.,
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
.

5.6
Proof of
Theorem

5.1
Transferring Probabilities

By
Lemma

5.18
, the composition polynomial
h
should have decent (anti-)concentration properties under
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R
.
To conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, we will need to “transfer” the (anti-)concentration properties back to the original polynomial
p
under
D=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\mathcal{P}
.
In this subsection, our goal is to show that the probability of
p
being small/large under
D
is not too much larger than the probability of
h
being small/large under
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R
. The formal statement is given below.

Lemma 5.23
.

For any threshold
t>\delta^{M/2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>2t]\leq O(1)\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\mid R]+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right)+O(\delta^{N/2}).

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t]\leq O(1)\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<2t\mid R]+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right)+O(\delta^{N/2}).

Proof.

The proof of the above lemma is divided into two steps.

1.

We bound the probability of
p
being small/large under
D
by the probability of
h
being small/large under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R
.

2.

We bound the probability of
h
being small/large under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R
by the probability of
h
being small/large under
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R
.

We start with the first step. The key observation is that if we define
\tilde{p}(\mathbf{x})=p(\mathbf{x})-e(\mathbf{x})=h(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x}))
, the distribution of
\tilde{p}(D)
is actually exactly the same as
h(\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R)
.
Therefore, it suffices to control the influence of the error polynomial
e
on the probability.
To show that, we will exploit the fact that the error polynomial
e
has its
L_{2}
-norm bounded by
\delta^{M}
, which is tiny when
M
is sufficiently large, and has good concentration under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
. As a side remark, this is precisely the source of the additive term
\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right)
in
Lemma

5.23
.

Lemma 5.24

(Transfer (Anti-)Concentration Probabilities)
.

For any threshold
t>\delta^{M/2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}[h(\mathbf{y})<2t\mid R]+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>2t]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}[h(\mathbf{y})>t\mid R]+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

Proof.

We start by showing a concentration bound of
e
under the conditional distribution
D
. In particular, for
t>\delta^{M/2}
, we show that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|e(x)\right|>t\right]<\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

(44)

We know that
e
has good concentration under the full Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
By
Lemma

3.15
, with some slight reparameterization, we have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|e(x)\right|>t\right]<\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

Since we assume that the conditioning set has mass at least
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
, the tail bound gets magnified by at most a factor of
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(1/\epsilon)
after the conditioning. This gives us

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|e(x)\right|>t\right]<\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(1/\epsilon)\;\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right)<\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}+O_{d,\ell}(\log(1/\epsilon))\right).

Notice that when
t>\delta^{M/2}
, the expression in the exponential function is
still
\Omega\left(t\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}
when
M
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
. This concludes the proof of Inequality (
44
).

We now proceed to show the first inequality in
Lemma

5.24
.
By our decomposition, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t]=\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|f(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})))+e(\mathbf{x})\right|<t].

We claim that the event
A:=\left|f(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})))+e(\mathbf{x})\right|<t

implies the event
\left|f(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x}))\right|<2t

or

\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>t
.
To show this, for the sake of contradiction, we assume that
\left|f(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x}))\right|>2t

and

\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|<t
. Then by the triangle inequality, we would have
\left|f(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})))+e(\mathbf{x})\right|>t
, which contradicts with event
A
.
With the implication in mind, we can apply the union bound:

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t]

\displaystyle\leq\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|h(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x}))\right|<2t]+\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>t]

\displaystyle=\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<2t\mid R]+\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>t].

(45)

Bounding the second term
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}[\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>t]
via
Equation

44
then concludes the proof of the first inequality.
The argument for the second inequality is symmetric to the first one, and is therefore omitted.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.24
.
∎

∎

For the second step, we need to exploit the fact that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is close in total variation distance to some distribution
\mathcal{M}
, which is comparable to
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
.
One technical issue is that we actually want to bound the conditional probabilities of the relevant distributions on the set
R
, which will magnify the distribution distances between them.
This is where we use our assumption that the mass of
R
is not too light under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
in the statement of our theorem, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]=\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[q_{i}(\mathbf{x})\in I_{i}\forall i\in[\ell]\right]>\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
.
With this assumption in mind, it is then easy to see that the total variation distance is increased to at most
\delta^{N/2}
if we take
N
to be sufficiently large given
d,\ell
.

Claim 5.25
.

Suppose that
N
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
.
The surrogate distribution
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R
is
\delta^{N/2}
-close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{M}\mid R
.

Proof.

We know that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is
O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{M}
(see
Section

5.5
for their definitions).
Hence, their probability masses over
R
differ by at most
O(\delta^{N})
.
By the assumption of
Theorem

5.1
, we know that the mass of
R
under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is at least
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)=\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,K}(\delta)
(by the definition of
\delta
such that
\delta^{d^{2}K}=\epsilon
).
Therefore, when
N
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
, it follows that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R
is
\delta^{N/2}
-close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{M}\mid R
.
∎

Then we know that the probability of any set under
\mathcal{M}\mid R
is within a constant factor of
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R
, since
\mathcal{M}
and
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
are comparable to each other.

Claim 5.26
.

For any set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}[\mathbf{x}\in S\mid R]\leq O(1)\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}[\mathbf{x}\in S\mid R]+O(\delta^{N/2}).

From here,
Lemma

5.23
readily follows from
Lemma

5.24
and
Claim

5.26
.

5.7
Proof of
Theorem

5.1
Relating the Norms

From
Lemma

5.27
, we know that the probability of
p
being smaller/larger than a threshold
t
under
D
can be appropriately bounded by the probability of
h
being smaller/larger than the same threshold
t
(up to a constant factor) under
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
(plus some additive terms that can be ignored if we take
M,N
to be sufficiently large).
However, notice that in the standard formulation of (anti-)concentration inequalities, the size of the polynomial should be normalized by its
L_{2}
-norm under the corresponding distribution.
Therefore, before we can conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, we need to show that the
L_{2}
norm of
h
under
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta|R
is on the same scale as that of
p
under
D
.

Lemma 5.27
.

It holds

\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}=\Theta(1)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

5.23
, we will divide our proof into steps:

\displaystyle\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}=\Theta(1)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}\,,

(46)

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}=\Theta(1)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M}\mid R,L_{2}}\,,

(47)

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M}\mid R,L_{2}}=\Theta(1)\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}.

(48)

For the first step, we start with the simple observation that

\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}
and
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}
differ by at most
\left\|e\right\|_{D,L_{2}}
, which is a tiny term since
\left\|e\right\|_{D}\leq\delta^{M}
.
This can be easily shown using the triangle inequality and the definition of these distributions/polynomials.

Claim 5.28
.

It holds
\left|\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}-\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}\right|\leq\delta^{M/2}
.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\left|\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}-\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}\right|\leq\left\|e\right\|_{D,L_{2}}.

Recall that
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{M}
by the guarantees of
Theorem

4.1
,
D:=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\mathcal{P}
, and the mass of
\mathcal{P}
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
is at least
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
by our assumption.
Moreover,
\epsilon,\delta
satisfy the equality
\delta^{d^{2}K}=\epsilon
.
Hence, when
M
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
, it follows that

\left\|e\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\leq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(1/\epsilon)\delta^{M}\leq\delta^{M/2}
.
∎

Eventually, our goal is to argue that the
L_{2}
-norms of interest are within a constant factor of each other.
This additive bound in
Claim

5.28
will be sufficient for this purpose given that the conditional
L_{2}
norm, i.e.,
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}
,
\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}
, is not much smaller than the corresponding unconditional versions, i.e.,
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}
,
\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}
.
It turns out that we can take advantage of the fact that these polynomials, i.e.,
p,h
, are decently anti-concentrated
to argue that the conditioning cannot trivialize their
L_{2}
norms.

Lemma 5.29
.

It holds

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}=\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon).

Proof.

It holds

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}\geq\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[(h\circ\mathbf{q})^{2}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}\right]\,,

where we denote by
h\circ\mathbf{q}
the composition of
h
and
\mathbf{q}
, i.e.,

h\circ\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})=h(\mathbf{q}_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,\mathbf{q}_{m}(\mathbf{x}))
.
By the guarantees of
Theorem

4.1
,
h\circ\mathbf{q}
is a degree at most
d
polynomial.
Define

\kappa:=\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]
.
By the Carbery Wright Theorem (
Lemma

3.14
), we have that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|(h\circ\mathbf{q})(\mathbf{x})\right|<(\kappa/C)^{d}\left\|h\circ\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}}\right]>\kappa/2\,,

for some sufficiently large universal constant
C
.
By the union bound, we hence have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|\left(h\circ\mathbf{q}\right)(\mathbf{x})\right|>(\kappa/C)^{d}\left\|h\circ\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}},\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]>\kappa/2.

Hence, we can derive the lower bound

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\kappa^{-1}\;\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[(h\circ\mathbf{q})^{2}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}\;\mathbbm{1}\{\left|\left(h\circ\mathbf{q}\right)(\mathbf{x})\right|>(\kappa/C)^{d}\left\|h\circ\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\kappa^{-1}\;\frac{\kappa}{2}\;(\kappa/C)^{d}\left\|h\circ\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}}\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}\,,

where in the last inequality we use that
\kappa\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)

and that
\left\|h\circ\mathbf{q}\right\|_{L_{2}}=\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}
.
Using almost identical arguments, one can show the second inequality in
Lemma

5.29
as well.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.29
.
∎

Proof of Step(1) (
Equation

46
).

From the definitions of
h,\mathbf{q},p,e
and the triangle inequality, we have

\left|\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}-\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}\right|\leq\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta^{M}.

Since we assume that
\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}=1
, it follows that
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}=\Omega(1)
.
Then
Lemma

5.29
gives us
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}=\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\gg\delta^{M}
, when
M
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
.
Combining this with
Claim

5.28
then concludes the proof of Step (1) (
Equation

46
).
∎

We now turn to Step (2) (
Equation

47
). If it were the case that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))=\mathcal{M}
, Step (2) would be trivial.
The situation becomes more complicated when
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
is only close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{M}
.
In order to relate the
L_{2}
norms of
h
under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
and under
\mathcal{M}
, we need to exploit the fact that
h
satisfies good concentration under
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
and under
\mathcal{M}
.

Claim 5.30
.

For any threshold
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}\right]<O\left(2^{-t^{2/d}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}\right]<O\left(\exp\left(-\left(t\epsilon^{1/K}\right)^{1/d}\right)\right).

Proof.

We note that the distribution of
h(\mathbf{x})
, where
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
, is the same as the distribution of

h\circ\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})
, where
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
. By the guarantees of
Theorem

4.1
, we know that
h\circ\mathbf{q}
is a degree at most
d
polynomial.
Hence, the first inequality simply follows from the concentration of polynomials under the standard Gaussian (
Lemma

3.15
).

For the second inequality, we will exploit the fact that
\mathcal{M}
is comparable to
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}\right]\leq 2\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t/2\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right].

Recall that each
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
is a
\big(\delta,\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log(1/\delta))\big)
-reasonable Gaussian. Therefore,
using the concentration property of the reasonable Gaussian mixture
(
Lemma

5.18
)
14
14

14

We apply the lemma with the conditioning set
S
being the entire space.
, we further have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>t/2\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right]\leq O(1)\;\exp\left(-\Omega_{d,m}\left(\delta^{2d}\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log^{-1}(1/\delta))\;t\right)^{1/d}\right).

When
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m,N
, we thus have

\Omega_{d,m}\left(\delta^{2d}\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log^{-1}(1/\delta))\right)\geq\delta^{2d+1}>\epsilon^{1/K}.

This concludes the proof of the second inequality.
∎

When a polynomial satisfies good concentration under a distribution, we can take advantage of this concentration property to control the contribution of a small fraction of the points from the distribution to the
L_{2}
norm of the polynomial under the distribution.

Claim 5.31

(
L_{2}
-Norm Stability)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Let
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{L}
be two distributions satisfying

\alpha\mathcal{E}\leq\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),\alpha\mathcal{L}\leq\mathcal{M}

15
15

15

Recall the notation
D\leq D^{\prime}
for two distributions
D,D^{\prime}
means that the probability density function of
D
is pointwise bounded above by that of
D^{\prime}
.
.
Then it holds

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{L},L_{2}}^{2}<O_{d}(\alpha)\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{E},L_{2}}^{2}<O_{d}(\alpha)\;\log^{d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

Using
Claim

5.30
, we know that for any positive integer
i
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|>\epsilon^{-1/K}\;i^{d}\;\log^{d}(1/\alpha)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}\right]<C\;\alpha^{i}\,,

(49)

for some universal constant
C
.
We will use
F_{i}
to denote the set

F_{i}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ such that }\epsilon^{-1/K}i^{d}\;\log^{d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}\leq\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|\leq\epsilon^{-1/K}(i+1)^{d}\;\log^{d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}\}.

In particular, each
F_{i}
is constructed such that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}]\leq C\alpha^{i}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\alpha\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\alpha\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\alpha\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\right]\right)\epsilon^{-2/K}\;(i+1)^{2d}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\right]\epsilon^{-2/K}\;(i+1)^{2d}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}+C\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha^{i}\;(i+1)^{2d}\;\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\,,

where in the first inequality we bound
h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\}

from above by
(i+1)^{2d}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}
using the definition of
F_{i}
,
in the second inequality
we bound
\alpha\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\right]
from above by

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\mathbf{x}\in F_{i}\right]
using the fact that

\alpha\;\mathcal{E}\leq\mathcal{M}
, and in the last inequality we use
Equation

49
.
Now observe that the series

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha^{i}(i+1)^{2d}
is converging and of order
O_{d}(\alpha)

Hence, the entire expression is bounded above by

O_{d}(\alpha)\;\epsilon^{-2/K}\;\log^{2d}(1/\alpha)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}
.
This concludes the proof of the first inequality in
Claim

5.31
.
The second inequality can be shown in an almost identical way and we therefore omit its proof.
This concludes the proof of
Claim

5.31
.
∎

The fact that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))|R
and
\mathcal{M}|R
are close in total variation distance allows us to view them as “noisy versions” of each other. Combining this with
Claim

5.31
allows us to relate the
L_{2}
-norms of
h
under them.

Proof of Step(2).

We first argue the “unconditional version” of the lemma. In particular, we claim that

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}=\Theta(1)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}.

(50)

Since
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
and
\mathcal{M}
are
O(\delta^{N})
-close in total variation distance, we have the equality

\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))-\alpha\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{M}-\alpha\mathcal{L}\,,

where
\alpha=O(\delta^{N})
,
\alpha\mathcal{E}\leq\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
, and

\alpha\mathcal{L}\leq\mathcal{M}
.
Using the definition of
L_{2}
-norm, we thus have

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}-\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{E},L_{2}}^{2}=\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}-\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{L},L_{2}}^{2}.

Recall that
\alpha=O(\delta^{N})
.
Hence, when
N
is sufficiently large
and
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,K
, applying
Claim

5.31
gives us

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{E},L_{2}}^{2}\leq\delta^{N/2}\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}\,,\,\alpha\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{L},L_{2}}^{2}\leq\delta^{N/2}\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}.

(51)

We therefore have

\left|\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}-\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\right|\leq\delta^{N/2}\left(\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\right).

Since
\delta^{N/2}=o(1)
,
Equation

50
hence follows.

Next we turn to the conditional
L_{2}
-norms.
Notice that even if we restrict to the set
R
, we still have the equality

\displaystyle\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]-\alpha\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]-\alpha\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{L}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right].

It is easy to see that
\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]
is at most
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{E},L_{2}}^{2}
(and similarly for
\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{L}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]
).
Combining this with
Equation

51
gives us

\displaystyle\left|\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]-\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]\right|\leq\delta^{N/2}\left(\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\right).

Equation

50
implies that
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2},\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}
are within a constant factor of each other.
Hence, we can further simplify the right hand side of the inequality above, which gives us

\displaystyle\left|\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]-\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{E}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]\right|\leq O\left(\delta^{N/2}\right)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}.

Define
\kappa:=\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))}\left[\mathbf{x}\in R\right]
.
By our assumption, we have
\kappa\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
.
We can normalize the above inequality with
\kappa
, which leads to

\displaystyle\left|\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\right]-\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]/\kappa\right|\leq\delta^{N/4}\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}.

(52)

We are almost done here except that we have the wrong normalization factor for
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M}\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}
. Fortunately, the current normalization factor
\kappa
is not too different from
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}{\mathbf{x}\in R}
.
Due to the fact that
\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))
and
\mathcal{M}
are close in total variation distance, we must have
\left|\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\mathbf{x}\in R\right]-\kappa\right|\leq O(\delta^{N})
.
This gives us

\displaystyle\left|\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]/\kappa-\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}\mid R}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{N/2}\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[h^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{x}\in R\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{N/2}\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M},L_{2}}^{2}\leq O\left(\delta^{N/2}\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}\,,

(53)

where in the last inequality we again use
Equation

50
.
Combining
53
,
Equation

52
and the triangle inequality then gives us

\left|\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}-\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M}\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}\right|\leq O\left(\delta^{N/4}\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}^{2}.

Lastly, by
Lemma

5.29
,
\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})),L_{2}}
is at most
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(1/\epsilon)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\mathbf{q}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}))\mid R,L_{2}}
.
When
N
is sufficiently small,
\delta^{N/4}\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(1/\epsilon)=o(1)
. This then allows to conclude that the two terms on the left hand side of the above inequality are within a constant factor of each other.
∎

Finally, we show Step (3) (
Equation

48
), which easily follows from the definition of distribution comparability.

Proof of Step (3) (
Equation

48
).

By the definition of distribution comparability, there exists a constant
C
such that

1/C\int_{\theta}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mid R\right)(\mathbf{x})\;d\theta\leq\mathcal{M}\mid R(\mathbf{x})\leq C\int_{\theta}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mid R\right)(\mathbf{x})\;d\theta

for all
\mathbf{x}
.
We hence have

\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{M}|R,L_{2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\mathcal{M}\mid R\right)(\mathbf{x})h^{2}(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}\leq C\;\int_{\mathbf{x}}\int_{\theta}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mid R\right)(\mathbf{x})\;d\theta\;h^{2}(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}=C\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}^{2}.

The other direction can be shown similarly.
∎

Combining the three steps (
Equation

46
,
Equation

47
, and
Equation

48
) then concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.27
.

5.8
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

We are now ready to combine everything together to derive our final (anti-)concentration bounds.
We will show the anti-concentration and the concentration bounds separately.
A crucial fact that we will use is that the
L_{2}
-norm of
p
under the original distribution
D
is within constant factors of the
L_{2}
-norm of the surrogate polynomial
h
under the conditional reasonable Gaussian mixture
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
, as shown in
Lemma

5.27
.
Specifically, there exists a sufficiently large universal constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\left\|f\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\leq\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}.

(54)

Proof of Anti-concentration.

Choose some
t
satisfying
\epsilon<t<\epsilon^{2/K}
.

Equation

54
implies

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\;C\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\right].

Notice that the threshold used in the right hand side of the equation can be bounded below by

t\;C\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}>\epsilon\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}>\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)=\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\delta^{d^{2}K})>\delta^{M/2}\,,

where the first inequality uses
Equation

54
and our choice of
t>\epsilon
,
the second inequality uses
Lemma

5.29
, and the last inequality is true as long as
M
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell,K
.

Lemma

5.23
then allows us to relate the probability of the event under
D
to that under the mixture
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
, which implies that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<C\epsilon\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\pr_{\mathbf{y}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{y})\right|<2Ct\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\right]+O(\epsilon^{N/(2d^{2}K)})+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

We bound the three terms separately.
For the first term, since we know that
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R
is a mixture of
(\delta,\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log(1/\delta)))
-reasonable Gaussians conditioned on the axis-aligned rectangle
R
, where each coordinate is at most
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\log(1/\delta))
-far from the origin, invoking
Lemma

5.18
gives us the bound

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{x})\right|<2Ct\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,m}(1)\;\left(t\delta^{-2d}\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,N}(\log(1/\delta))\right)^{1/d}.

Recall that
\delta^{d^{2}K}=\epsilon
and
t<\epsilon^{2/K}
.
Hence,
O_{d,m}(1)\;\delta^{-2d}\ll t^{-1/2}
when
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,m
.
It then follows that the first term is bounded above by
t^{1/(2d)}/2
.
Since
\epsilon<t
, the second term is obviously a lower-order term when
N
is a sufficiently large multiple of
d^{2}K
.
For the third term, we have already shown that

t\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta\mid R,L_{2}}\geq\delta^{M/2}
.
Hence, it is also obviously a lower-order term when
M
is a sufficiently large constant multiple of
d^{2}K
.
Combining the bounds for the three terms then conclude the proof of anti-concentration:

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<Ct\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right]\leq t^{1/(2d)}.

Proof of Concentration.

Finally, we turn our attention to the concentration bound.
Notice that the relevant bound needs to hold for all
t>0
.
A technical issue is that the concentration bound we can establish via comparing
D
to
\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta
will stop working in the extremely far tail of the distribution since we inevitably incur a cost of
O(\delta^{N})
due to the application of
Lemma

5.23
.
Fortunately, in the extreme tail region, it is enough for us to argue through the concentration of
p
under the full Gaussian.
Specifically, we need to divide into two cases depending on the value of the threshold
t
.

Case I: Extreme Tails.

In the first case, we have
t>\delta^{-N/K}
.
We have already shown that
\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
.
Hence, it follows that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2},D}\right]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}\right].

In this case, it suffices to argue through the concentration of the polynomial under the full Gaussian.
In particular, using
Lemma

3.15
, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2},D}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\Omega(t\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon))^{1/d}\right).

Using the fact that the mass of the conditioning set of
D
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})

is at least
\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,K}(\epsilon)
, we further have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2},D}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\Omega(t\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon))^{1/d}+O_{d,\ell,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))\right).

Since we assume that
t>\delta^{-N/d}
, when
N
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell
, and
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,\ell,K,N
,
it holds

-\Omega(t\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon))^{1/d}+O_{d,\ell,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))\geq t^{1/(2d)}
.
Combining these inequalities together then gives us

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2},D}\right]\leq\exp(-t^{1/2d})<t^{-K}.

Case II: Moderate Tails.

In the second case, we will choose
1<t<\delta^{-N/d}
(as the concentration bound is trivial for
t<1
).
In this case, the proof is similar to the one for anti-concentration.
In particular, using
Equation

54
, we have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\epsilon^{-1/K}\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left(\epsilon^{-1/K}/C\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right].

Since

t\left(\epsilon^{-1/K}/C\right)\;\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}>\Omega\left(\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right)>\delta^{M/2}
, we can again apply
Lemma

5.23
, which gives us

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left(\epsilon^{-1/K}/C\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\pr_{\mathbf{y}\sim\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta}\left[\left|h(\mathbf{y})\right|>\frac{t\epsilon^{-1/K}}{2C}\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\right]+O(\delta^{N/2})

\displaystyle+\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{t\epsilon^{-1/K}}{2C}\left\|h\right\|_{\int_{\theta}\mathcal{N}_{\theta}d\theta,L_{2}}\delta^{-M}\right)^{1/d}\right).

(55)

For the first term, using the concentration bound for a mixture of reasonable Gaussians, we have that it is bounded above by

\exp\left(-\Omega\left(t\epsilon^{-1/K}\frac{\delta^{2d}}{\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\log(1/\delta))}\right)^{1/d}\right).

Notice that
\epsilon^{-1/K}\frac{\delta^{2d}}{\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\log(1/\delta))}\gg 1
since
\delta^{d^{2}K}=\epsilon
and
\delta
is sufficiently small given
d,\ell
.
Thus, the probability can be bounded above by
\exp(-\Omega(t)^{1/d})<O_{d,K}(t^{-1/K})
.
For the second term, since we assume that
t<\delta^{-N/K}
, it then follows that

O(\delta^{N/2})<O\left(t^{-1/K}\right)
.
For the last term, we already have that the expression inside
\Omega(\cdot)
is at least
\epsilon^{M/2}
.
Hence, the term can be bounded above by
\exp\left(\delta^{-M/(2d)}\right)
, which is a lower order term compared to
O(\delta^{N/2})
.
Combining the bounds on the three terms on the right hand side of
Equation

55
then gives us

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\epsilon^{-1/K}\left\|p\right\|_{D,L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,K}\left(t^{-K}\right).

This concludes the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

6
Polynomial Set Partitioner

The fundamental barrier against a naive localization strategy that iteratively applies the robust margin-perceptron algorithm on the low-margin points with respect to the polynomial learned from the previous iteration is the following: the Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on the set
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|\leq\epsilon\}
does not necessarily satisfy Carbery-Wright type anti-concentration.
Equipped with
Theorem

4.1
and
Theorem

5.1
, we can build an efficient routine in to decompose a poorly anti-concentrated set roughly into
\mathrm{poly}_{d}(1/\epsilon)
many disjoint sets such that the conditional distribution on each subset satisfies good anti-concentration (see
Theorem

2.1
).

Theorem

2.1
allows us to at least kick-start our second round of perceptron learning by running it on each of the conditional distributions independently.
To make sure we can keep doing so after multiple rounds of perceptron learning, we need the routine to be capable of decomposing sets under not only the standard Gaussian distribution but also under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
