conditioned on a previous set it has produced.
Thankfully, this is made possible by the fact that the super non-singular decomposition algorithm (see
Theorem

4.1
) is “extendible”.
Specifically, assume that we have received a second polynomial
p^{\prime}
after conditioning on the set
\{\mathbf{x}:(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}))\in R\}
for some
\ell
-dimensional axis-aligned rectangle
R
. We can then extend
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
into a larger super non-singular set
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
such that we can still approximately write
p^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\approx h^{\prime}(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))
.
We can similarly cover
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\left|p^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\right|\leq\epsilon,(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}))\in R\}
with sets of the form
\{\mathbf{x}:(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{\ell}(\mathbf{x})\in R,(q_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x})\in R^{\prime})\}
, where
R^{\prime}
is some other
(m-\ell)
-dimensional axis-aligned rectangle.

However, after each partitioning iteration, the new sets produced will be defined by polynomials with weaker super non-singular properties.
This is because each time after we extend some super non-singualar set using
Theorem

4.1
, the resulting set produced will still be super non-singular but with slightly worse parameters.
Hence, to ensure our overall localization procedure can be used iteratively in sufficiently many rounds and that the resulting set of polynomials is still sufficiently super non-singular to establish the desired (anti-)concentration properties through
Theorem

5.1
,
we need the initial set of polynomials produced to satisfy quite strong super non-singular conditions.
To make it easier to express the strength of super non-singularity needed in each round, we define the notion of
Level-
k
super non-singular
polynomials with respect to a tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M)\in(0,1)\times\left(\mathbbm{Z}^{+}\right)^{3}
(these parameters encode the input to our algorithm and will be fixed in all rounds during its execution).
Intuitively, Level-
k
super non-singularity means that the underlying set can still be extended
k
more times and the resulting set will still be sufficiently super non-singular.

Definition 6.1

(Level-
k
Super Non-Singular Polynomial Set)
.

Fix a tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M)\in(0,1)\times\left(\mathbbm{Z}^{+}\right)^{3}
.
We use the integers from the tuple to define an infinite sequence of functions

\{f_{d,K,M}^{(0)},\cdots,f_{d,K,M}^{(k)},\cdots\}.

Let
f_{d,K,M}^{(0)}:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be a function defined as
f_{d,K,M}^{(0)}(\ell)=C_{d,\ell,K}
, where
C_{d,\ell,K}
is a number that is sufficiently large given its subscripts as required by
Theorem

5.1
.
Then we define the remaining functions in the sequence recursively as

f_{d,K,M}^{(k)}(\ell)=C_{d,\ell,f^{(k-1)}_{d,K,M},M}\,,

where
C_{d,\ell,f^{(k-1)}_{d,K,M},M}
is a number that is sufficiently large given its subscripts as required by
Theorem

4.1
.
Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a degree-
d
polynomial transformation.
We say
\mathbf{q}
is Level-
k
super non-singular with respect to
(\epsilon,d,K,M)
if
\mathbf{q}
is
\left(\epsilon^{1/(3^{k+1}d^{2}K)},f_{d,K,M}^{(k)}(m)\right)
-super non-singular.

Before presenting the formal statement of the main result of the section, we discuss the growth rate of the functions defined in
Definition

6.1
.

Claim 6.2
.

Fix a tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M)\in(0,1)\times\left(\mathbbm{Z}^{+}\right)^{3}
.
Let
f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}
be the
k
-th function in the function sequence defined in
Definition

6.1
.
Then it holds
C_{d,\ell,f^{(k)}_{d,K,M},M}=O_{d,\ell,k,K,M}(1)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the number
C_{d,\ell,f^{(k)}_{d,K,M},M}
can be computed by a program that only takes
d,\ell,k,K,M
as input.
We know that there exists a program that can compute the number
C_{d,\ell,f^{(k)}_{d,K,M},M}
given oracle access to the function
f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that
f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}(\ell)
for any positive integer
\ell
can be computed by a sub-routine that only takes
d,k,K,M,\ell
as input.
We show this via induction on
k
.
For
k=0
, we have that
f^{(0)}_{d,K,M}(\ell)=C_{d,\ell,K}
, which can be computed by a program that takes input
d,\ell,K
.
For
k\geq 1
, we have that

f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}(\ell)=C_{d,\ell,f_{d,K,M}^{(k-1)},K}
.
Therefore, we know that
f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}(\ell)
can be computed by a program given
d,\ell,K
as input and oracle access to
f_{d,K,M}^{(k-1)}
.
By our inductive hypothesis,
f_{d,K,M}^{(k-1)}(\ell^{\prime})
for any positive integer
\ell^{\prime}
can be computed by a program that takes only the input
d,k,K,M,\ell^{\prime}
.
Thus, we conclude that
f^{(k)}_{d,K,M}(\ell)
can be computed by a program given
d,k,K,M,\ell
.
By mathematical induction, this holds for all positive integers
k
. This concludes the proof of
Claim

6.2
.
∎

Now we are ready to state the formal guarantees of
Polynomial-Set-Parititioner
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
k,d,\ell,K
be positive integers and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Define the following mathematical objects:

•

p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a degree-
d
polynomial with
\left\|p\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq 1
.

•

\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
is a vector-valued polynomial of degree at most
d
.

•

R
is an axis-aligned rectangle in
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.

Assume that (i)
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,\ell,k,K
(ii)
\mathbf{q}
is Level-
k
super non-singular with respect to the tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M=3)
, and (iii)
\pr[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
.
Then for some
m=O_{d,\ell,k,K}(1)
, there exists an algorithm
Polynomial-Set-Partitioner
that takes the set

\mathcal{P}:=\{\mathbf{x}:\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\text{ and }\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}

as input and produces at most
\epsilon^{-5md}
many sets of the form

\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}:=\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\}\,,

where
\bar{\mathbf{q}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a degree-
d
vector-valued polynomial and each
\bar{R}_{i}
is an
m
-dimensional axis-aligned rectangle.
In particular, the sets produced satisfy the following:

1.

The distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on each
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
satisfies strong (anti-)concentration properties. Specifically, for any polynomial
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
of degree-
d
, for
\epsilon<t<\epsilon^{2/K}
,
it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}}\left[\left|g(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\;\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i},L_{2}}\right]\leq t^{1/(2d)}\,,

and for all
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}}\left[\left|g(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\epsilon^{-1/K}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i},L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,K}\left(t^{-K}\right).

2.

Each set
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
has non-trivial probability mass under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\right]>\epsilon^{3+5md}\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

3.

Define
\bar{\mathcal{P}}:=\bigcup_{i}\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
.
The points from the given set
\mathcal{P}
that are not included in
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
have small probability mass, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\not\in\bar{\mathcal{P}},\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}\right]<\epsilon^{2}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

4.

\bar{\mathcal{P}}
is approximately contained in a slightly larger set compared to
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.

\tilde{\mathcal{P}}:=\{\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<2\epsilon,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
. In particular, it holds

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in\bar{\mathcal{P}},\mathbf{x}\not\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\right]<\epsilon^{2}\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

5.

\bar{\mathbf{q}}
is level-
(k-1)
super non-singular with respect to the tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M=3)
.

Moreover, the algorithm runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(1/\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Let
S=\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell}\}
be the set of polynomials corresponding to the coordinates of
\mathbf{q}
.
By our assumption,
\mathbf{q}
is Level-
k
super non-singular with respect to the tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M=3)
.
Thus, by definition, the set
S
is
(\epsilon^{1/(3^{k+1}d^{2}K)},f_{d,K,M=3}^{(k)}(\ell))
super non-singular.
Since
f_{d,K,M=3}^{(k)}(\ell)=C_{d,\ell,f^{(k-1)},M=3}
by its definition,
using
Theorem

4.1
,
we can extend the set into
another
(\epsilon^{1/(3^{k}d^{2}K)},f^{(k-1)}_{d,K,M=3}(m))
super non-singular set

\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{\ell},q_{\ell+1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
,
where
m=O_{d,\ell,f^{(k-1)}_{d,K,M=3}}(1)=O_{d,\ell,k,K}(1)
by
Claim

6.2
, such that there exists a composition polynomial
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and an error polynomial
e:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{x})=h(q_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\cdots,q_{m}(\mathbf{x}))+e(\mathbf{x})\,,\,\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\epsilon^{3}.

(56)

Moreover, we can find the decomposition in
Equation

56
in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,f^{(k-1)}_{d,K,M=3},M=3}(1/\epsilon)
,
which is simply
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(1/\epsilon)
by
Claim

6.2
.

Define
\bar{\mathbf{q}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
to be the vector-valued polynomial whose
i
-th coordinate is
q_{i}
.
Then by definition,
\bar{\mathbf{q}}
is Level-
(k-1)
super non-singular with respect to the same tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M=3)
.
This guarantees Condition (5) in the proposition is satisfied.

With the decomposition and its associated polynomial transformation
\bar{\mathbf{q}}
in our hand, we begin our search of axis-aligned rectangles in the
m
-dimensional output space of
\bar{\mathbf{q}}
.
The first step is to restrict our search space to
a very large rectangle that is obtained by extending the original
\ell
-dimensional rectangle
R
in all the remaining
(m-\ell)
dimensions:

\bar{R}:=R\times[-\alpha_{\ell,d}\log^{d/2}(m/\epsilon),\alpha_{\ell,d}\log^{d/2}(m/\epsilon)]^{m-\ell}\,,

where
\alpha_{\ell,d}
is some number that depends on
\ell,d
that we will specify later.
Since we have
\left\|q_{i}\right\|_{L_{2}}=1
for all
i\in[m]
,
by the union bound and the concentration of polynomials (
Lemma

3.15
), it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\not\in\bar{R}\right]<\exp\left(-\Omega(\alpha_{\ell,d})^{2/d}\log(1/\epsilon)\right).

Since we also assume that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]>\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
, it is not hard to see that if we choose
\alpha_{d,\ell}
to be sufficiently large given
d,\ell
, we then have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\not\in\bar{R}\right]<\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

(57)

We next partition the rectangle
\bar{R}
into small rectangles
\{\bar{R}_{i}\}
, each of side length
\epsilon^{4d}
.
Since the side length of the rectangle
\bar{R}
is
2\alpha_{d,\ell}\log^{d/2}(m/\epsilon)=\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))
(recall that
m=O_{d,\ell,k,K}(1)
),
it is not hard to see that
the number of sub-rectangles we end up with is at most

\left(\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))\;\epsilon^{-4d}\right)^{m}\leq\mathrm{poly}_{d,m,k,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))\;\epsilon^{-4dm}\leq\epsilon^{-5md}\,,

where the last inequality is true if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,m,k,K
.
These will be the candidate rectangles we use to define the output set
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}
be the empirical distribution of
\mathcal{V}=\mathrm{poly}_{m,d}(1/\epsilon)
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
Among the small rectangles, we pick the ones that are (i) not too light, and (ii) contain a significant fraction of points satisfying
\left|p(\mathbf{x})<\epsilon\right|
. More formally, the selection rule is given by

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\tilde{\mathcal{N}}}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]>2\epsilon^{3+5md}\;,\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\tilde{\mathcal{N}}}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\mid\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\right]>2\epsilon^{3}.

Note that this step can be completed in time
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}(\mathcal{V})\;\epsilon^{-5md}
. The final output of the algorithm will simply be
\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{i}=\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{q}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\}
for those
\bar{R}_{i}
that are retained after the selection.
Hence, this shows that the total runtime is
\mathrm{poly}(n^{d})\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(1/\epsilon)
.

We proceed to show the sets produced satisfy Conditions (1)-(4) in
Proposition

6.3
.
Let
\mathcal{K}
be the family of sub-rectangles kept and
\mathcal{T}
be the family of sub-rectangles discarded after the selection procedure.
If we take
\mathcal{V}=\mathrm{poly}_{m,d}(1/\epsilon)
many samples,
with high probability, we will have the following probability mass separation between the rectangles from
\mathcal{K}
and from
\mathcal{T}
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.

For all the sub-rectangles
R^{(i)}\in\mathcal{K}
picked, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]>\epsilon^{3+5md}\text{ and }\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\mid\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\right]>\epsilon^{3}.

(58)

For all the sub-rectangles
R^{(i)}\in\mathcal{T}
removed, we have

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]<3\epsilon^{3+5md}\text{ or }\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\mid\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in{\bar{R}}^{(i)}\right]<3\epsilon^{3}.

(59)

Notice that
Equation

58
establishes Condition (2) in
Proposition

6.3
.

For each
\bar{R}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
, we claim that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
conditioned on
\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}
satisfies good (anti-)concentration.
Indeed, the mass of the set under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
is at least
\epsilon^{3+5d}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]\geq\mathrm{poly}_{d,m}(1/\epsilon)
by
Equation

58
, and the set
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{m}\}
is at least

\left(\epsilon^{1/(3^{k+1}d^{2}K)},f_{d,K,M}^{(k)}(m)\right)
-super non-singular.
As long as
k\geq 0
, we then have
\epsilon^{1/(3^{k+1}d^{2}K)}\geq\epsilon^{1/(3d^{2}K)}
, and

f_{d,K,M}^{(k)}(m)\geq C_{d,m,K}
.
Hence, (anti-)concentration properties of the conditional distribution follows from
Theorem

5.1
.
This establishes Condition (1) in
Proposition

6.3
.

We next verify that the union of the produced sets, i.e.,
\bigcup_{\bar{R}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}}\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R^{(i)}\}
, contains almost all the points from
\{\mathbf{x}:\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\,,\,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
.
Throughout our algorithm, a point
\mathbf{x}
may be missed due to three different reasons.
First, it may be removed if it does not lie in the giant rectangle where we begin our search, i.e.

\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\not\in\bar{R}
.

Equation

57
implies that the probability mass of these points under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
is at most

\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]
.
Secondly, the point may be thrown if it lies in some small rectangle
\bar{R}_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
.
By
Equation

59
, there are two subcases.
In the first subcase, this rectangle is thrown due to its probability mass being too small, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\right]<3\epsilon^{3+5md}\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

In total, there are at most
\epsilon^{-5md}
light rectangles.
So the total probability mass of
\mathbf{x}
discarded in this case is bounded above by

3\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
In the second subcase, we have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon|\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\right]<3\epsilon^{3}.

This is equivalent to

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<\epsilon\,,\,\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\right]<3\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\right].

By construction, the union of all rectangles
\bar{R}_{i}
gives rise to
\bar{R}
. Moreover, since the first
\ell
dimensions of
\bar{R}
and
\bar{\mathbf{q}}
are identical to those of
R
and
\mathbf{q}
respectively, it is not hard to see that the set
\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}\}
is a subset of
\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
.
Consequently, the total mass of the points removed due to this reason is also no more than

3\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]
under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
In summary, the total mass discarded from the original set
\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
is no more than

O(\epsilon^{3})\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]<\epsilon^{2}\;\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]

under
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
.
This establishes Condition (3) in
Proposition

6.3
.

Finally, we verify that the extra points
outside the set
\{\mathbf{x}:\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<2\epsilon,\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
included in

\bigcup_{\bar{R}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}}\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\}
have small probability mass.
It is clear that all the sets produced are subsets of
\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
by the construction of the sub-rectangles
\bar{R}_{i}
’s.
Hence, it remains to show that the value of
p
evaluated at these subsets is not too big.
Recall that the polynomial
p
can be written as

p(\mathbf{x})=h(\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x}))+e(\mathbf{x})

by our decomposition.
Fix some
\bar{R}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
.
By our selection rule of
\mathcal{K}
(
Equation

58
), we know that there is at least some
\mathbf{x}^{*}
in the set
\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\}
such that

\left|h(\mathbf{q}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{*}))+e(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|=\left|p(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|<\epsilon.

Consider another point
\mathbf{y}\in\{\mathbf{x}:\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x})\in\bar{R}_{i}\}
.
Since
R^{(i)}
has side-length
\epsilon^{4d}
by construction and its coordinates are at most

\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))
far from the origin, we know that

\left\|-\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{y})\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m}\epsilon^{4d}
and

\max\left(\left\|\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right\|_{2},\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{y})\right)\leq\sqrt{m}\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}(\log(1/\epsilon))
.
Moreover, by the guarantees of
Theorem

5.1
, we have that

\left\|h\right\|_{L_{2}}<\epsilon^{-3d-1}
.
Notice that
h
is a degree-
d
, dimension-
m
polynomial.
Hence,
Corollary

3.24
implies that

\left|h(\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*}))-h(\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{y}))\right|\leq O_{d,m}(1)\;\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell,k,K}\left(\log(1/\epsilon)\right)\;\epsilon^{-3d-1}\;\epsilon^{4d}<\epsilon^{2}\,,

as long as
\epsilon
is sufficiently small given
d,\ell,k,K
.
It suffices to argue that
\left|e(\mathbf{y})-e(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|
is not too large.
If it were the case that
\left|e(\mathbf{y})-e(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|<\epsilon^{2}
, we would then have

\left|p(\mathbf{y})\right|\leq\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|+\left|h(\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{x}^{*}))-h(\bar{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{y}))\right|+\left|e(\mathbf{y})-e(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|\leq\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2})<2\epsilon.

Then the produced sets would be
exactly
contained in the set
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}=\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R,\left|p(\mathbf{x})\right|<2\epsilon\}
.
Unfortunately, this is not true in general.
However, since
\left\|e\right\|_{L_{2}}<\epsilon^{3}
by the guarantees of
Theorem

4.1
, we can show that
the mass of the points which do not satisfy
\left|e(\mathbf{y})-e(\mathbf{x}^{*})\right|<\epsilon^{2}
is small.
In particular, by
Lemma

3.15
, we know that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>\alpha_{d,\ell}^{d/2}\log^{d/2}(1/\epsilon)\epsilon^{3}\right]\leq\epsilon^{\Omega(\alpha_{d,\ell})}.

Recall that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]>\mathrm{poly}_{d,\ell}(\epsilon)
by our assumption.
Hence, when
\alpha_{d,\ell}
is sufficiently large given
d,\ell
, we will have

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|<\alpha_{d,\ell}^{d/2}\log^{d/2}(1/\epsilon)\epsilon^{3}<\epsilon^{2}\right]>1-\epsilon^{3}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right].

Since the only additional points that are included in the sets produced but excluded from
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
are those satisfying
\left|e(\mathbf{x})\right|>\epsilon^{2}
, it is not hard to conclude that Condition (4) in the proposition is satisfied.
We have verified all the properties listed in the proposition.
This concludes the proof of
Proposition

6.3
.
∎

7
Learning Polynomial Threshold Functions using Super Non-Singular Polynomials

In this section, we present an efficient algorithm that learns an unknown polynomial threshold function under the Gaussian distribution in the presence of an
\mathrm{opt}
fraction of adversarial corruptions, and achieves error
O_{c,d}(\mathrm{opt}^{1-c})
for any desirable constant
c>0
.
The formal statement of our theorem is as follows:

Theorem 7.1

(Main Algorithmic Result, Formal Statement)
.

Let
\epsilon,\mathrm{opt},\eta,c\in(0,1),d\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Let
D
be the distribution of
(\mathbf{x},y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{\pm 1\}
, where
\mathbf{x}
follows the standard Gaussian distribution, and
y
is the output of some unknown degree-
d
PTF evaluated at
\mathbf{x}
.
Given an
\mathrm{opt}
-corrupted version of a set of
n^{O(d)}\mathrm{poly}_{d,c}(1/\epsilon)\log(1/\eta)
i.i.d. samples from
D
,
there exists an algorithm, with computation complexity at most
n^{O(d)}\mathrm{poly}_{d,c}(1/\epsilon)\log(1/\eta)
, that computes a decision list
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\{\pm 1\}
consisting of at most
\mathrm{poly}_{d,c}(1/\epsilon)
degree-
d
PTFs such that, with probability at least
1-\eta
, it holds

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[h(\mathbf{x})\neq y]\leq A_{c,d}~\mathrm{opt}^{1-c}+\epsilon\,,

where
A_{c,d}>0
is an absolute constant depending only on
c,d
.

We now provide an overview of the algorithm.
The high-level framework is to implement an iterative localization strategy through the application of the Polynomial-Set-Partitioner from
Proposition

6.3
developed in
Section

6
.
Roughly speaking, by applying the Polynomial-Set-Partitioner, we can approximately partition an unclassified area defined by polynomial transformations into subsets such that the Gaussian distribution conditioned on each subset within the partition satisfies good (anti-)concentration properties.
Once such a partition is identified, we will next run a robust margin perceptron algorithm (see
Algorithm

1
) on each of the conditional distributions.
Under good (anti)-concentration properties,
the margin perceptron algorithm can output a PTF such that the prediction is accurate for all but
\epsilon^{\Omega(1/d)}
fraction of low-margin points under
\epsilon
-fraction of adversarial corruptions (see
Proposition

7.3
).
Consequently, combining the PTFs learned from different conditional distributions then gives a hypothesis capable of accurately classifying all but an
\epsilon^{\Omega(1/d)}
fraction of data points within the region.
The process continues
iteratively: the algorithm is given a new unclassified region and applies the perceptron update until the
remaining unclassified mass is reduced to a negligible fraction, specifically
O_{c,d}(\mathrm{opt}^{1-c})
.

Before proceeding with the proof of
Theorem

7.1
, we first present our robust margin-perceptron algorithm. This routine is the main ingredient of our algorithm. Given a distribution that satisfies some good concentration and anti-concentration properties,
Algorithm

1

robustly estimates a weak learner that classifies almost all the points.

Input:
Sample access to an at most
\epsilon
-corrupted oracle of the distribution
D
; access to
\mathbf{x}
-marginals
D_{\mathbf{x}}
, see
Definition

1.1
,
K>0
.

Parameters:

F=\epsilon^{1-8/\sqrt{K}},\gamma=\epsilon^{4/\sqrt{K}}
and
C>0
a sufficiently large absolute constant.

Output:
A vector
\mathbf{w}
and a region
B
so that:
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[\mathrm{sign}({\mathbf{w}}\cdot\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid B]\leq C_{K}(\epsilon)^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})}
and
B=\{(\mathbf{x},y)\mid y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}),|\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{w}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
.

1.

t\leftarrow 0
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{q}^{(t)}
be an estimator of
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[y\mathbf{x}]
using the algorithm of
Fact

7.4
.

3.

Let
B_{t}=\{(\mathbf{x},y)\mid|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
and
B_{t}^{\prime}=\{(\mathbf{x},y)\mid y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}),|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}

4.

While
\pr[B_{t}^{\prime}]\geq 2F
and
t<O(\epsilon^{-C})
, do

(a)

Let
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
be an estimator of
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[y\mathds{1}\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x})\}\mathbf{x}\mid(\mathbf{x},y)\in B_{t}]
using the algorithm of
Fact

7.4
.

(b)

\mathbf{q}^{(t+1)}=\mathbf{q}^{(t)}+\lambda_{t}\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
, where
\lambda_{t}=(1/4)\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\pr[B_{t}^{\prime}]\gamma/\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}
.

(c)

t\leftarrow t+1
.

5.

Return
\mathbf{q}^{(t)}
.

Algorithm 1

Perceptron Update

Remark 7.2
.

In what follows, we will overload our norm notation and for a vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we denote by
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{p}}
its distributional
L_{p}
norm when it
is treated as a linear function of
\mathbf{x}
, i.e.,

\|\mathbf{w}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{p}}=\Big(\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}
. We continue to denote the common norm of
\mathbf{w}

by
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{p}=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\mathbf{w}_{i}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

when we treat it as a vector of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
K\in\mathbbm{Z}_{+}
,

C,C_{1}>0
, and
D
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{\pm 1\}
.
Assume the following statements are true.

1.

The samples
\mathbf{x}
are labeled with respect to an unknown function
f(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{x})
for some
\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

2.

The covariance matrix of the marginal distribution
D_{\mathbf{x}}

satisfies
\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}]\preccurlyeq 2\mathbf{I}
.

3.

The
\mathbf{x}
-marginal distribution
D_{\mathbf{x}}
satisfies anti-concentration, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\leq t\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\leq t^{C}\,,

for some number
C>0
, and for all
t\in(\epsilon,\epsilon^{2/{\sqrt{K}}})
.

4.

D_{\mathbf{x}}
has concentration, i.e.,

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|>t\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\leq C_{1}t^{-K}\,,

for some
C_{1}>0
and all
t>0

Then
Algorithm

1
given access to an
\epsilon
-corrupted version of
\mathrm{poly}(n/\epsilon)
i.i.d. samples from
D
, runs in sample-polynomial time, and with probability at least
2/3
computes a weight vector
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
such that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[\mathrm{sign}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid B]\leq C_{1}^{1/K}~O_{C,K,d}(\epsilon^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})})\,,

where
B=\{\mathbf{x}:|\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{w}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
for
\gamma=\epsilon^{4/\sqrt{K}}
.

Proof.

We set the parameters
\gamma=\epsilon^{4/\sqrt{K}}
and
F=\epsilon^{1-8/\sqrt{K}}
. We denote by
D^{c}
the distribution
D
that is at most
\epsilon
-corrupted.
First, we show that we can robustly estimate
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[y\mathbf{x}]
. We will use of following fact.

Fact 7.4

(Proposition 2.2 of
[
DKS18
]
)
.

Fix
\epsilon,\xi\in(0,1)
and let
D
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[-1,1]

corresponding to pairs labeled according to some function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto[-1,1]
, i.e.,
(\mathbf{x},f(\mathbf{x}))\sim D
.
Assume that
D
satisfies the following: (i) concentration, i.e.,
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|>t\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\leq Ct^{-K}
, for some
C>0
and for all
t>0
, (ii) bounded variance in each direction, i.e.,
\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}]\preccurlyeq\Sigma
.
Then, there is an algorithm that given an
\epsilon
-corrupted set
S
from
D
with
|S|=O(n\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon,1/\xi))
and a description of the
\mathbf{x}
-marginals
D_{\mathbf{x}}
, runs in
\mathrm{poly}(|S|)
time and outputs
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that
\|\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[f(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}]-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\leq c\|\Sigma\|_{2}C^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
with probability at least
1-\xi
, where
c>0
is an absolute constant.

For the rest of the proof, let
c>0
be the absolute constant from
Fact

7.4
.
Note that for our distribution and for any band
B=\{\mathbf{x}:|\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{w}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
, we have that the distribution
D^{c}
conditioned on
B
is at most
O(\epsilon/\pr[B])=O(\epsilon)
corrupted, because
\pr[\mathbf{x}\in B]\geq 1-\gamma^{C}=1-\epsilon^{Cc}=\Omega(1)
.
Let
\mathbf{q}^{(0)}
be a robust estimator (given by
Fact

7.4
) of
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[y\mathbf{x}]
with error at most
cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
, i.e.,
\|\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[y\mathbf{x}]-\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\|_{2}\leq cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
. We first show that
\mathbf{q}^{(0)}
is well-correlated with
\mathbf{w}^{\ast}
.

Claim 7.5
.

It holds that
\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\geq(1/2)\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}
.

Proof.

Using that
\mathbf{q}^{(0)}
is equal to
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[f(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}]
with error at most
cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
, we get that

\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\geq\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{x}|]-cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}\;.

Using the anti-concentration, i.e., property (ii) of
Proposition

7.3
, we get that

\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{x}|]\geq\rho(1-\rho^{C})
. We can assume without loss of generality that
\epsilon
is less than a sufficiently small constant depending on
C,K
and
C_{1}^{1/K}
. Otherwise, the constant hypothesis is
O(\epsilon)
. Hence, it holds that
1-\epsilon^{C/\sqrt{K}}\geq 1/2
. It follows that for
\rho=\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}
, we have that
\rho(1-\rho^{C})\geq\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}/2
, and therefore

\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\geq\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}/2
.
∎

Denote as
B_{t}=\{|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
.
While the mass of the region
B_{t}^{\prime}=\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}),|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}
is larger than our
threshold or the maximum number of
iterations have not exceeded our threshold (Step (
4
)), our algorithm updates the current hypothesis using the following update rule:

\mathbf{q}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathbf{q}^{(t)}+\lambda_{t}\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\;,

where
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
is a robust estimator of
\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\mid B_{t}]
, where
f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=f(\mathbf{x})\mathds{1}\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x})\}
, obtained by the algorithm of
Fact

7.4
.
Let
F_{t}=\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[(\mathbf{x},y)\in B_{t}^{\prime}]\geq F
(see Step (
4
)) and denote by
D_{t}
the distribution
D
conditioned on the event
B_{t}
. Note that the true mass of the region
B_{t}
with respect to the distribution
D
is at least
1/2
.
We claim that the distribution
D_{t}
satisfies the assumptions of
Fact

7.4
. First, note that the corruption level of
D_{t}
is at most
2\epsilon
, furthermore
for the distribution
D_{t}
, it holds that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim(D_{t})_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|>t\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\leq 2\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|>t\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\leq 2C_{1}t^{-K}
. Moreover, for any unit vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that
\E_{\mathbf{x}\sim(D_{t})_{\mathbf{x}}}[(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x})^{2}]\leq 4
. Therefore,
D_{t}
satisfies the assumptions of
Fact

7.4
but with parameters
C=2C_{1}
,
\|\Sigma\|_{2}\leq 4
and at most
2\epsilon
corruption rate.

Using
Fact

7.4
on the distribution
D_{t}
, we have that for our robust estimator
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
, it holds that
\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}-\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\mid(\mathbf{x},y)\in B_{t}]\|_{2}\leq 8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
.
We show that by choosing appropriately the stepsize
\lambda_{t}
, we decrease the norm of
\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}
in each iteration. We show the following:

Claim 7.6
.

Assume that
\lambda_{t}=(1/2)\gamma F_{t}/\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}
. Then it holds that
\|\mathbf{q}^{(t+1)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\leq\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}(1-(1/16)F_{t}^{2/K}\gamma^{2}/C_{1}^{2/K})^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Note that by definition, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}\mid B_{t}]+8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\mathds{1}\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x})\}\mid(\mathbf{x},y)\in B_{t}]+8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq-(\gamma F_{t}-8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K})\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\leq-(\gamma F_{t}/2)\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\;,

(60)

where we used that
\gamma F_{t}\geq\epsilon^{1-2/\sqrt{K}}\geq 8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
for all
F_{t}\geq F
.
Next, we calculate the norm of
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
. We have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}=1}\E_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D_{t}}[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\mathds{1}\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x})\}]\leq 2C_{1}^{1/K}F_{t}^{1-1/(K-1)}\;,

where in the last inequality we used the concentration of the tails.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{q}^{(t+1)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}+2\lambda_{t}\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{(t)}+\lambda_{t}^{2}\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}-\lambda_{t}\gamma F_{t}\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}+4C_{1}^{2/K}F_{t}^{-2/K}\lambda_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}-\lambda_{t}(F_{t}\gamma/2)\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}(1-(1/16)F_{t}^{2/K}\gamma^{2}/C_{1}^{2/K})\;,

where we used that
\lambda_{t}=(1/2)\gamma F_{t}/\|\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}^{2}
.
∎

Finally, we show that
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
does not decrease the correlation with
\mathbf{w}^{\ast}
by a lot. We show the following

Claim 7.7
.

It holds that
\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\geq-8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{p}^{(t)}
is a robust estimator of
\E[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\mid|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]
with error
8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}
and it holds that
\E[f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{x}\mid|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}]\geq 0
, therefore we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{(t)}\geq-8cC_{1}^{1/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}\;.

∎

From
Claim

7.6
, we have that after
t=\Theta(\log(1/\epsilon))C_{1}^{2/K}/(\gamma^{2}F^{2/K})
iterations, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\leq\|\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}(1-\epsilon^{C_{3}/(2K)+2/K})^{t/2}\leq\epsilon^{1/2}\;.

On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{q}^{(t-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}+\lambda_{t}\mathbf{p}^{(t-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{q}^{(0)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{\ast}-8cC_{1}^{3/K}\epsilon^{1-1/K}t\gamma/F^{1-2/(K-1)}

\displaystyle\geq(1/2)\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}-C^{\prime}C_{1}^{3/K}\epsilon^{(1-1/K)(8/\sqrt{K})}\log(1/\epsilon)

\displaystyle\geq(1/2)\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}-C^{\prime}C_{1}^{3/K}\epsilon^{3/\sqrt{K}}\geq(1/4)\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}\;,

where
C^{\prime}>0
is an absolute constant and we used that
\gamma=\epsilon^{4/\sqrt{K}}
and
F=\epsilon^{1-8/\sqrt{K}}
, therefore we get that

(1/4)\epsilon^{2/\sqrt{K}}\leq\epsilon^{1/2}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, after at most
O(\log(1/\epsilon)/(\epsilon^{c_{3}}))
iterations for some absolute constant
c_{3}>0
, we have that
\E[\mathds{1}\{y\neq\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}),|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}]\leq F=\epsilon^{1-8/\sqrt{K}}
. To conclude the proof, note that by the anti-concentration assumption, we have that

\E[\mathds{1}\{|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\cdot\mathbf{x}|\geq\gamma\|\mathbf{q}^{(t)}\|_{D_{\mathbf{x}},L_{2}}\}]\geq 1-\gamma^{C}=1-O(\epsilon^{4C/\sqrt{K}})\geq 1/2
and the result follows. This completes the proof of
Proposition

7.3
.
∎

Input:
Access to at most
\mathrm{opt}^{1/\sqrt{K}}
-corrupted samples from a distribution
D
,
\mathrm{opt},\epsilon,K>0
.

Output:
A
d
-degree PTF
h
and interval
I
so that:
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[h(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid I]\leq O_{d,K}(\mathrm{opt}_{I}^{1-1/\sqrt{K}})+\epsilon\,,
and a list of unclassified regions
Q
.

Parameters:

c>0
sufficiently small absolute constant.

1.

Let
Z=\{\epsilon,\ldots,\mathrm{opt}^{O(1/\sqrt{K})}\}
.

2.

For each
\epsilon^{\prime}\in Z
, do

(a)

Run
Algorithm

1
for
\log(1/\eta)
times with parameters
\epsilon^{\prime},K
and get
T=\{(p_{1},I_{1})\ldots(p_{\log(1/\eta)},I_{\log(1/\eta)})\}
.

(b)

For each
(p,I)\in T
, if
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I]\leq O(\epsilon^{\prime 1-8/\sqrt{K}})
, then keep
(p,I)
and stop the search.

3.

Use
Proposition

6.3
and output a list
Q
of unclassified regions with respect to
(p,I)
.

4.

Return
(p,I),Q
.

Algorithm 2

Partial-Classifier

As outlined in our discussion, our algorithm partitions the unclassified regions into more manageable sub-regions.
In particular, the partition ensures that the resulting distributions conditioned on each of the sub-regions
satisfy good (anti-)concentration properties.
To facilitate this, we introduce the following definition, which characterizes the attributes of each subregion.
Specifically, given a polynomial transformation
\mathbf{q}
and a rectangle
R
we want the normal distribution conditional on
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R
to have both good anti-concentration and concentration behavior, while
\mathbf{q}
is also level-k
super non-singular.

Definition 7.8

((Anti-)concetrated Polynomial Slab)
.

Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and a rectangle
R\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. We say that a tuple
(\mathbf{q},R)
is an
(\epsilon,k,K,d,\ell,\zeta)
-(anti-)concentrated if the following properties are satisfied:

1.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:q(\mathbf{x})\in R\}
, then for any polynomial
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
of degree-
d
, for
\epsilon<t<\epsilon^{2/K}
,
it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid{\mathcal{P}}}\left[\left|g(\mathbf{x})\right|<t\;\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid{\mathcal{P}},L_{2}}\right]\leq t^{1/(2d)}\,,

and for all
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid{\mathcal{P}}}\left[\left|g(\mathbf{x})\right|>t\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})\mid{\mathcal{P}},L_{2}}\right]\leq O_{d,K}\left(\epsilon^{-1}t^{-K}\right)\;.

2.

The mass of
\mathcal{P}
is lower bound by
\zeta
, i.e.,
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}[q(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq\zeta
.

3.

\mathbf{q}
is level-
k
super non-singular
with respect to the tuple
(\epsilon,d,K,M=3)
.

Our subsequent proposition demonstrates that
Algorithm

2
classifies a region that fulfills the (anti-)concentration property of
Definition

7.8
.
Algorithm

2
employs
Algorithm

1
to categorize the majority of points effectively. Subsequently, it decomposes any remaining unclassified region into a list of smaller subregions, each inheriting the (anti-)concentration property
of
Definition

7.8
.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
n,k,d,\ell,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
\epsilon,\mathrm{opt},\eta\in(0,1)
. Let
D
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{\pm 1\}
such that (i)
D
is at most
\mathrm{opt}
-corrupted with respect an unknown
d
-degree PTF, (ii) the
\mathbf{x}
-marginal of
D
follows standard
n
-dimensional normal.
Let
(\mathbf{q},R)
be
(\mathrm{opt},k,K,d,\ell,\zeta)
-(anti-)concentrated. Let
D_{Q}
be the distribution conditioned on
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R
.
There exists an algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) that draws
N=O(n^{d})\mathrm{poly}_{K,d}(d,1/\epsilon)\log(1/\eta)
at most
\mathrm{opt}^{1/\sqrt{K}}
-corrupted samples from
D_{Q}
, runs in sample-polynomial time
and with probability at least
1-\eta
computes:

1.

A set of
m
-dimensional vector-valued polynomial
\mathbf{q^{\prime}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
where
m=O_{d,\ell,K}(1)
and a set of
m
-dimensional axis-aligned rectangles
\{R^{(1)},\ldots R^{(t)}\}
with
t\leq\mathrm{opt}^{-2m}
satisfying

(a)

For each
R^{(i)}
, the tuple
(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},R^{(i)})
is
(\mathrm{opt},k-1,K,d,m,\zeta)
-(anti-)concentrated.

(b)

For the union of the sets
S:=\bigcup_{i}\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\in R^{(i)}\}
, it holds that

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in S\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R\right]<\mathrm{opt}^{c/(2d)}\,.

2.

An at most
d
-degree polynomial
p
and a region
I=\{q(\mathbf{x})\in R\cap S^{c}\}
such that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D_{Q}}[\mathrm{sign}(p(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I]\leq C_{d,K}\mathrm{opt}_{I}^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})}+\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon\,,

for some absolute constant
C_{d,K}>0
.

Proof.

From
Definition

7.8
, for any
\rho\in(\mathrm{opt},\mathrm{opt}^{2/K})
, we have that for any polynomial
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, it holds

\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{Q}}\left[p(\mathbf{x})<\rho\;\|p(\mathbf{x})\|_{D_{Q},L_{2}}\right]\leq\rho^{1/(2d)}.

(61)

In what follows for a tensor
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes k_{m}}
we define
\mathbf{A}^{\flat}
its flattening to a vector of
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\cdots k_{m}}
.
Let
\mathbf{z}=((\mathbf{x},1)^{\otimes d})^{\flat}
and denote as
D_{\otimes}
the distribution of
\mathbf{z}
. Notice that the
\mathbf{z}
contains a basis for all polynomials of degree at most
d
, so the dimension of
\mathbf{z}
is
(n+1)^{d}
. Then for any vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)^{d}}
, we have that
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{z}
is an at most
d
-degree polynomial, therefore we have that for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)^{d}}
it holds that

\pr_{\mathbf{z}\sim D_{\otimes}}\left[|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{z}|<\rho\;\|\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{z}\|_{D_{\otimes},L_{2}}\right]\leq\rho^{1/(2d)}.

We first show that we can transform
\mathbf{z}
so that the covariance of
\mathbf{z}
is equal to identity.
Note that
\E[\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{\top}]
is Hermitian matrix, so it can be written as
\E[\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{\top}]=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{P}^{\top}
,
for some diagonal matrix
\mathbf{D}
and orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{P}
. Let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{D}^{-1/2}
and let
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{z}
.
It holds that
\E[\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})^{\top}]=\mathbf{I}
. Furthermore, note that if
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{z}
is a polynomial before the transformation, then
(\mathbf{T}^{-1}\mathbf{v})\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{z}
. Therefore all the polynomials can be expressed in the new space. To calculate the
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{D}
, we can run standard Gram–Schmidt orthonormalization and get a
\gamma
-approximation (in
L_{2}
sense) of these matrices with
\mathrm{poly}(d/\gamma)
samples and time.

Denote by
\mathcal{C}
the at most
\mathrm{opt}^{1/\sqrt{K}}
-corrupted oracle to the distribution
D_{Q}
. Before we apply
Algorithm

1
to find a new hypothesis, we need to guess the corruption rate of
\mathcal{C}
. For this purpose, we construct a list
Z=\{\epsilon,2\epsilon,\ldots,\mathrm{opt}^{1/\sqrt{K}}\}
containing all the possible corruption levels. If
\mathrm{opt}_{Q}
is the true corruption level of
\mathcal{C}
, then there exists
\mathrm{opt}^{\prime}\in Z
, such that
\mathrm{opt}_{Q}\leq\mathrm{opt}^{\prime}\leq\mathrm{opt}_{Q}+2\epsilon
.
Therefore, for the correct noise rate, from
Proposition

7.3
using
Algorithm

1
with probability at least
2/3
, we get a polynomial
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and a set
I=\{|p(\mathbf{x})|\geq 2\mathrm{opt}_{Q}^{4/\sqrt{K}}\}
so that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D_{Q}}[\mathrm{sign}(p(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I]\leq C_{K,d}(\mathrm{opt}_{I}+\epsilon)^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})}\,.

By running the same procedure
\log(1/\eta)
and testing the result, we can amplify the probability of this procedure to
1-\eta
. Note that from
Equation

61
, we have that

\pr[\mathbf{x}\in I\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq 1-(\mathrm{opt}_{Q})^{2/(d\sqrt{K})}\geq 1-(\mathrm{opt})^{2/(dK)}\;,

(62)

where we used that
\mathrm{opt}_{Q}\leq\mathrm{opt}^{1/\sqrt{K}}
.
To classify the rest of the points in the set
\{q(\mathbf{x})\in R,\mathbf{x}\not\in I\}
, we need to split the region into smaller sets using
Proposition

6.3
.
From
Proposition

6.3
, given the polynomial
p
and the current polynomial vector
q
for some
m=O_{d,K,\ell}(1)
, we get the following: a new
m
-dimensional vector-valued polynomial
\mathbf{q^{\prime}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
and a set of
m
-dimensional axis-aligned rectangles
\{R^{(i)}\}
of size at most
\mathrm{opt}^{-2m}
satisfy so that the sets for each
i
, it holds that
(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},R^{(i)})
is
(\mathrm{opt},k-1,K,d,m,\epsilon^{3+5md}\zeta)
-(anti-)concentrated.
Moreover, let
S:=\bigcup_{i}\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{q}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\in R^{(i)}\}
, we have that
\pr[\mathbf{x}\in S,\mathbf{x}\in I\mid q(\mathbf{x})\in R]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{2}
and
\pr[\mathbf{x}\not\in S,\mathbf{x}\not\in I\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{2}
. Define
J=(I\cup S^{c})-S
. Note that for the region
J
it holds that
\pr[\mathbf{x}\in J\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq\pr[\mathbf{x}\in I\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]-\mathrm{opt}^{2}
and therefore, he have that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D_{Q}}[\mathrm{sign}(p(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in J]\leq C_{K,d}(\mathrm{opt}_{I}+\epsilon)^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})}+\mathrm{opt}\,.
and that
\pr[\mathbf{x}\in J\mid\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq 1-2(\mathrm{opt})^{2/(dK)}
.

∎

Input:
Access to at most
\mathrm{opt}
-corrupted samples from a distribution
D
over
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{\pm 1\}
, noise rate
\mathrm{opt}>0
, parameters
\epsilon,K>0

Output:
A decision list of PTFs
h
so that:
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[h(\mathbf{x})\neq y]\leq O_{d,K}(\mathrm{opt}^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})})+\epsilon
.

1.

Run
Algorithm

2
on
D
and get
h=\{(p,I)\}
,
Q
.

2.

While
Q
is not empty, do

(a)

(\mathbf{q},R)\leftarrow\mathrm{pop}(Q)
.

(b)

Let
D_{Q}
be the distribution
D
conditioned on
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})\in R
.

(c)

Run
Algorithm

2
on
D_{Q}
and get
\{(p,I)\}
,
Q^{\prime}
.

(d)

Let
Q=Q\cup Q^{\prime}
and
h=h\cup\{p,I\}
.

3.

Return
h
.

Algorithm 3

Combined Algorithm

7.1
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the
\mathrm{opt}
-corrupted oracle access to the distribution
D
. For any event
Q
, we denote
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
the oracle access to the distribution
D_{Q}
i.e., the distribution
D
conditioned on the event
Q
. We can simulate
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
from
\mathcal{C}
by accepting only points that satisfy the event
Q
. We denote as
\mathrm{opt}_{Q}
the corruption rate of the oracle
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
. Let
k
be a sufficiently large multiply of
d
and
C_{K}=O(1/\sqrt{K})
. We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{opt}
is less than an absolute constant
C^{\prime}=O_{d,K}(1)
Otherwise, the constant hypothesis will have error
O(C^{\prime})
. Moreover, if
\epsilon>\mathrm{opt}
then we can take
\mathrm{opt}=\epsilon
as the error guarantee of the algorithm will be
O(\epsilon^{1-O(1/\sqrt{K})})
. Otherwise, we can assume that
\mathrm{opt}/2<\epsilon<2\mathrm{opt}
as we can find a
2
-approximation of
\mathrm{opt}
by re-running the algorithm
1/\epsilon
times.
The distribution is standard
d
-dimensional normal, hence, it satisfies the assumptions of
Proposition

7.9
for
\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{1}
and
R=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Therefore, from Step (
1
), we get a polynomial
p
and a region
I
so that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[\mathrm{sign}(p(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I]\leq C^{\prime}(\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon)^{1-C_{K}}\,.

First, note that our algorithm in Step (
2
), maintains a list of unclassified regions
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{\xi}
. In order to show that our algorithm works, we need to show that in each iteration, we decrease the unclassified region and we output a classifier that classifies accurately most of the points. We first show that we can remove the regions with noise rates more than
\mathrm{opt}^{C_{K}}
as their total mass contributes to the error at most
\mathrm{opt}^{1-C_{K}}
. Denote with
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{l}
the regions in
Q
so that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p^{*}(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid\Xi_{i}]\geq(\mathrm{opt})^{C_{K}}.

We show that
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[\mathbf{x}\in\cup_{i=1}^{l}\Xi_{i}]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{1-C_{K}}
.

Claim 7.10
.

It holds that
\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[\mathbf{x}\in\cup_{i=1}^{l}\Xi_{i}]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{1-C_{K}}
.

Proof.

Let
A\cup_{i=1}^{l}\Xi_{i}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{opt}

\displaystyle=\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p^{*}(\mathbf{x})\neq y]

\displaystyle=\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p^{*}(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in A]\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}\in A]+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p^{*}(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in\Xi_{i}]\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}\in\Xi_{i}]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{l}\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}[p^{*}(\mathbf{x})\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in\Xi_{i}]\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}\in\Xi_{i}]\geq\mathrm{opt}^{C_{K}}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim D_{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathbf{x}\in\cup_{i=1}^{l}\Xi_{i}]\;.

By rearranging the above inequality, the result follows.
∎

Next, we show that in each iteration, we remove a large mass and we classify optimally the points of this mass. Let
(\mathbf{q},R)
be the element, we pop from
Q
at Step (
2a
). For some
k\in\mathbb{Z},\ell>0
, the
(\mathbf{q},R)
is
(\epsilon,k,K,d,\ell,\epsilon)
-(anti-)concentrated. For this region one of two events can happen: Either the corrupted oracle
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
has a corruption rate more than
\mathrm{opt}^{C_{K}}
in which case Step (
2c
) gives a constant hypothesis in this region, or we are given
a partial classifier
(p,I)
and a new set of regions
\{\mathbf{q}^{\prime},R^{(i)}\}
that are
(\epsilon,k-1,K,d,m,\epsilon^{3+5md})
-(anti-)concentrated, for
m=O_{\ell,K}(1)
. For the partial hypothesis, we have from
Proposition

7.9
, that

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D_{Q}}[\mathrm{sign}(p(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I]\leq C_{K,d}^{\prime}\mathrm{opt}_{I}^{1-C_{K}}+\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon\,,

Note that
\pr[\mathbf{x}\in I\mid q(\mathbf{x})\in R]\geq 1-\mathrm{opt}_{I}^{c/(2d)}
, therefore in each step we remove at least
\pr[q(\mathbf{x})\in R](1-\mathrm{opt}_{I}^{c/(2d)})
mass.

Let
S_{i}
be the unclassified regions on depth
i
. This means that set
S_{i}
is a union of sets
R^{(i)}
that are constructed after
i
applications of Polynomial-Set-Partitioner. We show that after
t=O(dC_{K})
steps, we have classified all but
O(\mathrm{opt})
mass. Note that after
t
iterations, we have that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in S_{t}\right]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{C_{K}ct/(2d)}
, hence for
t=O(dC_{K})
this is of order
\mathrm{opt}
. Therefore, we can drop the remaining sets.
To analyze the correctness of our algorithm, we denote the following, let
\{(p_{1},I_{1}),\ldots,(p_{T},I_{T})\}
be the decision list classifier and let
\Xi
be the union of regions discarded because the corruption rate was more than
\mathrm{opt}^{C_{K}}
. We denote as
h(\mathbf{x})
the classifier that if
\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}
for some
i
then
h(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{sign}(p_{i}(\mathbf{x}))
.
We have that

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[h(\mathbf{x})\neq y\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathrm{sign}(p_{i}(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]

\displaystyle+\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in\Xi\right]+\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\not\in\cup_{i}I_{i}\right]\;.

We have shown that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathrm{sign}(p_{i}(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]\leq C_{K,d}^{\prime}(\mathrm{opt}_{I_{i}})^{1-C_{K}}+\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon
.
Therefore we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{T}\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathrm{sign}(p_{i}(\mathbf{x}))\neq y\mid\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,d}^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{T}(\mathrm{opt}_{I_{i}}^{1-C_{K}}+\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon)\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in I_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,d}^{\prime}\mathrm{opt}^{1-C_{K}}+\epsilon\;,

where the last inequality follows from the power means inequality. Furthermore, from
Claim

7.10
, we have shown that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\in\Xi\right]\leq\mathrm{opt}^{1-C_{K}}
. Finally, we have shown that
\pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})}\left[\mathbf{x}\not\in\cup_{i}I_{i}\right]=O(\mathrm{opt})
. Therefore,

\pr_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim D}\left[h(\mathbf{x})\neq y\right]\leq C_{K,d}^{\prime}(\mathrm{opt})^{1-C_{K}}+\epsilon\;.

This completes the proof of
Theorem

7.1
.
